The notice is the action's name in the past tense, generated: /datum/notice/fell, inserted, buckled, unbuckled, crossed, uncrossed, removed. A name that already reads as past tense (hit, stumbled_into, round_started) is kept. notice = overrides it, as the op action does. A subtype declared with a slash (hit/projectile) inherits its parent's fields; its proc and ACT_TRY token use an underscore (act_hit_projectile, hit_projectile).

Calling one. The generated act_fall() takes its fields positionally and typed, so DreamChecker checks the call. ACT_TRY(src, fall, T, M) expands to a table lookup first and builds the context only when something hooks the action:

var/datum/act/fall/F = ACT_TRY(src, fall, T, M)   // the final act, after every adjusts(); ACT_PASS (nothing hooks it and no listener wants its notice, nothing allocated); or null (refused or taken over)
if(!F)
	return
...do the fall to ACT_FINAL(F, landing, T) and ACT_FINAL(F, landed_on, M): the act's fields, not your locals...
act_done(F)                      // ends it ACT_COMMITTED, publishes /datum/notice/fell and releases the context; a no-op for ACT_PASS
// A path that decides not to finish:  act_cancel(F)  releases the context, ends it ACT_REFUSED and publishes it to nobody who has not asked.
  • ACT_PASS is one shared constant, so act_done(ACT_PASS) and act_cancel(ACT_PASS) do nothing. It is returned only when nothing hooks the action and no listener wants a notice of it, for any outcome: a listener for the committed notice counts as hooking it, so with only an on_notice listener ACT_TRY returns a real act, and act_done publishes the committed notice. It carries no fields, because nothing hooked the action and nothing could have adjusted it: ACT_FINAL(F, field, local) reads F.field from a real act and the caller's own local from ACT_PASS, so one line is right on both paths. Only a hook's handler reads the context's fields otherwise.
  • A lint requires act_done or act_cancel on every path of the proc that called ACT_TRY. Forgetting leaks the context and holds its references.
  • A FIXED action has no ACT_TRY: PUBLISH(E, x, args...) announces it (section 10).
  • A taken-over action returns null from ACT_TRY and ends ACT_REPLACED: its past-tense notice is not published to listeners that have not asked for replaced actions (below).

The world actions: move, z_change, cross, uncross, bump, stumbled_into, fall, thrown_hit, hit (by entry), irradiate, injure, body_status, insert, remove, equip, unequip, speak, emote, buckle, unbuckle, climb, slash and op.

Outcomes and notices

Every action ends with exactly one outcome, and the notice reaches a listener only for the outcomes it asked for.

OutcomeIt meansHow it ends
ACT_COMMITTEDIt happenedact_done(F); an op that committed (section 9)
ACT_REFUSEDA requirement, or the caller, said noA refusing needs; act_cancel(F); a failed op
ACT_REPLACEDSomething else happened insteadAn instead hook took it over
  • Default listeners hear committed actions only. on_notice(/datum/notice/hit/melee, parts...) runs for ACT_COMMITTED. A listener that wants more opts in: on_notice(..., outcome = ACT_REPLACED), or outcome = ACT_ANY for every one of them. on_notice and on_op share one vocabulary, ACT_COMMITTED | ACT_REFUSED | ACT_REPLACED | ACT_ROLL_FAILED | ACT_ANY, and values combine with |. ACT_ROLL_FAILED is a filter value only: an op whose chance() failed still ends ACT_COMMITTED with A.rolled = FALSE, a listener for ACT_COMMITTED hears only committed ops whose roll succeeded, and ACT_ANY is all four, failed rolls included. (OP_* is the vocabulary of an effect's report and nothing else, section 9.) So every "it happened" handler can trust that it did, and a takeover is not announced as a hit that landed.
  • The handler reads the outcome as A.outcome on its /datum/act/notice. WANTS(E, /datum/notice/x) and PUBLISH consider the outcome too, so an outcome nobody asked for allocates nothing.
  • An op is an action, so its outcome is the same enum (section 9): committed, refused or replaced, and a failed op (a runtime) is reported as ACT_REFUSED with the runtime logged. A failed chance() is ACT_COMMITTED with A.rolled = FALSE (section 9), which a listener reaches with ACT_ROLL_FAILED.

Action paths: how input becomes an op

Every client input first becomes a typed event in the input inbox (section 2). It resolves through the paths below when the inbox drains it, and a target that has gone stale by then is dropped with the gate's reason.

InputPath
Click, shift, ctrl, alt and middle click, drag-drop, clicking the held itemA gesture (GESTURE_CLICK, _SHIFT, _CTRL, _ALT, _MIDDLE, _DRAG, _SELF) with origin ORIGIN_CLICK. The actor's bind profile and current stance map it to an ordered list of intents.
Context menu, radial, command bar, screentipsThe ops that match, by key. A pick runs the op with origin ORIGIN_MENU, which every physical binding accepts. Ones that fail Require are shown greyed, with their reason: action_options(actor, target, held) returns each candidate's key, label, enabled flag and reason, and screentip_for(actor, target, held, gesture) returns the text for the op a click would run. Both evaluate lazily and are cached (see "Resolution cost"). A target's own menu() entries are listed here (channel, below).
UI buttonAn op with a ui_act() binding, origin ORIGIN_UI
Topic linkAn op with a topic("key", args...) binding, origin ORIGIN_UI
Gameplay ability, action button, keybindAn op with a menu(button =, bind =) binding: origin ORIGIN_VERB for the Abilities entry and the action button, ORIGIN_HOTKEY for the keybind. All of an actor's own menu ops are listed under one "Abilities" verb-panel entry, each with its action button and keybind. Only client, OOC and admin commands are verb_entry(), ADMIN_VERB or DEBUG_VERB (section 13); a layering lint rejects verb_entry under code/content.
Silicon remote use, spoken commands, telekinesisA silicon's remote use is a remote() binding (provider with AFF_CONTROL, authority AUTH_REMOTE_ACCESS). A recognised spoken command arrives as ORIGIN_VERB: the speech parser maps a phrase to an op key. Telekinesis is a provider with a reach and a line-of-sight requirement, below.
Acting on the container you are inside (struggling in a belly, a locker, a mech cockpit)An inside() op: reach policy REACH_INSIDE, and the target is the actor's container
AI controllers, scripted actorsperform_op(actor, target, key, held) or perform_intent(actor, target, INTENT_X, held): the same path, the same requirements, origin ORIGIN_AI and authority AUTH_AI. perform_intent() considers the ops with a physical binding and returns the key of the op that ran, or null. perform_op() reaches an op by key through whichever binding of the op accepts the origin it runs under, and origin defaults to ORIGIN_AI: every physical binding (hand(), tool(), item(), stack(), in_hand(), at_target(), inside()) accepts it, and runs with the provider and the reach gate a click would have; ai() accepts it too, and is the binding of an op that has no physical one, which then uses the op's own reach policy and no provider. An op whose bindings accept the origin given (a menu()-only ability called with ORIGIN_AI) is refused with the reason "no binding accepts that origin", so a controller passes origin = ORIGIN_VERB for an ability, or the ability also declares ai(). perform_op() returns a /datum/op_result (the op's pooled act is released before the call returns; see "What a caller gets back" under Contexts), whose outcome is an ACT_* value, null while the op still waits. Code acting for the game itself, a forced revert or a scripted step, passes origin = ORIGIN_SYSTEM, which every op accepts and the origin mask does not stop; it still runs Match, Require, Wait and Do.
Admin authorityperform_op(actor, target, key, held, authority = A): authority AUTH_ADMIN with an authority datum, origin ORIGIN_SYSTEM. It bypasses the actor gate. The op decides what it asks of it with needs(req_rights(R_X))

Origin, reach, provider, authority and channel

Five separate facts describe an input, and an op names only the ones it cares about. The presets at the end of this subsection give the usual combinations, so authors rarely write them. A gesture is the raw input, which the actor's bind profile and stance turn into an ordered list of intents.

OriginWhere the input arrived
ORIGIN_CLICKA click or a drag on the map or in the contents view
ORIGIN_UIA button in an open tgui window, or a topic link
ORIGIN_MENUA pick from the context menu or a radial menu: the player chooses an op by key from the ops that match a target
ORIGIN_VERBThe Abilities entry, an action button or a spoken command
ORIGIN_HOTKEYA keybind
ORIGIN_AIAn AI controller or a script acting for a mob
ORIGIN_SYSTEMThe engine or an admin acting for the game itself: a forced revert, a scripted step, an authority call

Masks: ORIGIN_ALL and ORIGIN_NONE. The actor's acts_via stat holds a mask of origins.

Reach policyThe spatial relation the op needs
REACH_ADJACENTThe target is within the reach of the provider that performs the op: a hand reaches 1, a spear 2, telekinesis its range. Measured through the containment ledger; a provider declared with line_of_sight = TRUE also needs a clear line to the target
REACH_RANGE(n)Within n tiles, whatever the provider reaches: the op supplies its own range (thrown and ranged ops), so the provider neither limits nor extends it
REACH_INSIDEThe target is the actor's container. No spatial test
REACH_VIEWThe target is in the actor's view: a camera feed, a remote console
REACH_ANYNo spatial test. A window's own access requirement decides. A policy an op names; a provider never declares it, because a provider's reach = is a number of tiles
AffordanceWhat a provider addsGiven by
AFF_MANIPULATEHandling things: pressing, opening, inserting, takingHands, grippers, telekinesis
AFF_HOLDCarrying any itemHands
AFF_HOLD_SMALLCarrying small items onlyGrippers
AFF_ATTACKStrikingHands, natural weapons, weapons with reach
AFF_CONTROLOperating controls without handling anythingThe AI and borg interface
AFF_OBSERVEActing as an observer: a ghost's click and menuThe observer mob
AFF_TOOL_<Q>Using a tool of quality Q (AFF_TOOL_WELDER, AFF_TOOL_CROWBAR), generated one per TOOL_*Any item with tool_quality(Q), held in a hand or selected as a borg module
AuthorityThe permission it is exercised under
AUTH_PHYSICALThe actor's own body: its hands and its presence at the target. The actor gate and the reach gate apply in full
AUTH_REMOTE_ACCESSAccess granted over a link: a silicon's interface to a machine, a console's remote control. Physical bays are ignored; the machine's own remote-access requirements (an AI-control wire, a lock) decide
AUTH_ADMINAn admin acting by an authority datum. Bypasses the actor gate
AUTH_AIAn AI controller acting for a mob. The mob's own gates apply, and the busy policy is BUSY_REFUSE

Channel. An op is on the actor's own channel or on the target's, and the channel is the type that declares it, so it needs no keyword. An op declared on the actor, or on a capability granted to it (an ability), is the actor's own: menu() lists it under Abilities and no reach test applies. An op declared on the target's type is the target's: object verbs (eject, rotate, flip) migrate to menu() entries on the target's type, appear in that target's context menu, and are subject to the reach policy of their preset.

IntentWhat the gesture means
INTENT_USETouch it, or apply the held item. The default
INTENT_ATTACKHarm it. The hostile stances put it first in the intent list
INTENT_TOGGLE, INTENT_OPEN, INTENT_EJECTFlip a state, open it, take its contents out
INTENT_DROP_ONTOSomething dragged onto the target
INTENT_EXAMINELook at it
INTENT_PRESENTShow the held item as a credential (presents(T))

Gestures: GESTURE_CLICK, _SHIFT, _CTRL, _ALT, _MIDDLE, _DRAG (the held or dragged atom is dropped onto the target) and _SELF (the held item used on itself). UI, topic, menu, speech and authority inputs carry no gesture and no intent: they name their op by key. Stances are today's I_* ids (I_HELP, I_HURT, ...), which the bind profile reads and stance(S...) and req_stance(S) test.

Binding (preset)OriginReachProvider affordanceAuthorityIntentTier
hand()ORIGIN_CLICK, ORIGIN_MENU, ORIGIN_AIREACH_ADJACENTAFF_MANIPULATEAUTH_PHYSICALINTENT_USE; INTENT_TOGGLE when the op toggles()NORMAL
tool(Q), any_of_tools(Q...)ORIGIN_CLICK, ORIGIN_MENU, ORIGIN_AIREACH_ADJACENTAFF_TOOL_<Q> of the held toolAUTH_PHYSICALINTENT_USEPART
item(T), stack(T, n)ORIGIN_CLICK, ORIGIN_MENU, ORIGIN_AIREACH_ADJACENTAFF_MANIPULATEAUTH_PHYSICALINTENT_USE (INTENT_PRESENT with presents())PART
in_hand()ORIGIN_CLICK, ORIGIN_MENU, ORIGIN_AIREACH_ADJACENT (the held item on itself)AFF_HOLDAUTH_PHYSICALINTENT_USENORMAL
at_target(filter)ORIGIN_CLICK, ORIGIN_MENU, ORIGIN_AIREACH_ADJACENTAFF_HOLDAUTH_PHYSICALINTENT_USENORMAL
inside()ORIGIN_CLICK, ORIGIN_MENU, ORIGIN_AIREACH_INSIDEAFF_MANIPULATE (the hand provider)AUTH_PHYSICALINTENT_USENORMAL
remote()ORIGIN_CLICK, ORIGIN_MENU, ORIGIN_UIREACH_VIEWAFF_CONTROLAUTH_REMOTE_ACCESSINTENT_USENORMAL
menu(button =, bind =)ORIGIN_VERB, ORIGIN_HOTKEY for the actor's own op; ORIGIN_MENU for a target's menu entryREACH_ANY for the actor's own op; the target's reach for a target menu entrynoneAUTH_PHYSICALnone: chosen by keyNORMAL
ui_act()ORIGIN_UIREACH_ANY (window access decides)noneAUTH_PHYSICAL or AUTH_REMOTE_ACCESSnone: chosen by keyNORMAL
topic("key", args...)ORIGIN_UIREACH_ANYnoneAUTH_PHYSICAL or AUTH_REMOTE_ACCESSnone: chosen by keyNORMAL
ai()ORIGIN_AIthe op's ownnoneAUTH_AInone: chosen by keyNORMAL

An op takes several bindings, and every binding runs the same Match, Require, Wait and Do. op(key, inputs(hand(), ui_act()), parts...) declares them together; extend(key, binds(ui_act())) adds one to an inherited op; extend(key, inputs(...)) replaces the whole set. A window button and a click on the same op therefore have the same requirements, waits, costs, notice and log, and a test pins that (section 19). A physical binding is also what perform_intent() and a controller's ORIGIN_AI go through, so ai() is written only for an op that has no physical binding and that a controller may call by key (an ability an AI uses, say).

Select parts change one column. origin(mask), reach(policy), by(affordances) and authority(mask) each replace what the binding implies (by(AFF_MANIPULATE | AFF_CONTROL); reach(REACH_INSIDE)); without them every set comes from the binding. stance(S...) is sugar for when(req_stance(S...)) and hostile() for answers(INTENT_ATTACK). gesture(G) pins a raw gesture and is rare.

Reach lives on the provider, in tiles. at_target() takes no range. A provider says how far it reaches, what it adds, what it can carry and under what authority. provides(..., reach = n) is a number of tiles: a hand 1, a spear 2, TK_RANGE, the design name of today's TK_MAXRANGE (15 tiles), and BORG_INTERFACE_REACH (7 tiles, the view range), which a silicon's interface offers only to a remote() op that narrows itself to reach(REACH_ADJACENT). A provider with no reach = supplies no tile reach, and serves only ops whose reach policy needs none (REACH_RANGE(n), which brings its own range, REACH_VIEW, REACH_ANY, REACH_INSIDE): the AI's interface is provides(AFF_CONTROL, authority = AUTH_REMOTE_ACCESS). REACH_ANY is a policy that an op names, never a value of reach =. There is no hand provider on /mob/living. A species with hands grants it, as hands() in its SPECIES_CAPABILITIES (hands() is provides(AFF_MANIPULATE | AFF_ATTACK | AFF_HOLD, reach = 1), and species_capabilities() grants it while the mob's species names that species), and a mob with no species declares its own providers: a carp's bite (natural_weapon), a borg's modules and interface, the AI's remote control. A borg, an AI or a carp therefore has no hand ops unless something gives it AFF_MANIPULATE, and a gripper or a telekinesis mutation does. A restraint is not the provider's business: the implicit req_capable() refuses with the restraint's reason. A lost limb is: hands() is gated by a condition over the body (when(PROC_REF(has_working_hand), provides(...))), so when the last working hand goes the provider detaches, the actor's provider set generation bumps, and hand ops stop being candidates ("You can't do that without hands."). A borg's selected module counts as its held item, and what a gripper carries is A.held, with the gripper as the provider. A module provides the affordances it declares: an item with tool_quality(Q) provides AFF_TOOL_<Q>, so a borg's welder module does the welder's tool ops and, having declared no AFF_MANIPULATE, none of the hand ops.

Which provider performs an op. When several providers give the affordance an op accepts and reach the target, the held item's provider comes first, then the actor's hand (the provider its species grants), then any other provider by the shortest reach that reaches. That is the provider in A.provider. Telekinesis is therefore used only when nothing nearer reaches.

Telekinesis. Telekinesis is a provider of AFF_MANIPULATE with a reach of TK_RANGE tiles and line_of_sight = TRUE, so it can do any hand op on a target it can see within range: open a door, press a button, take an item off a table, pull a cell from an open APC. Everything else still applies. It provides no tool or attack affordances, so tool ops and attacks need the real thing. Compartments still gate it (a closed or locked cover keeps the bay unexposed), and every requirement still runs (a locked APC refuses with its reason). An op that means body contact (a grab, CPR, a surgery step) says reach(REACH_RANGE(1)).

CAPABILITIES(/obj/item/gripper)   // excerpt: the same list is in 16.8
	provides(AFF_MANIPULATE | AFF_HOLD_SMALL, reach = 1, accepts = list(/obj/item/cell, /obj/item/circuitboard))
CAPABILITIES(/obj/item/melee/spear)
	provides(AFF_ATTACK, reach = 2)   // an item that extends reach
CAPABILITIES(/datum/mutation/telekinesis)   // granted to the mob while the mutation is active
	provides(AFF_MANIPULATE, reach = TK_RANGE, line_of_sight = TRUE)   // no tool or attack affordances
CAPABILITIES(/mob/living/silicon/ai)
	provides(AFF_CONTROL, authority = AUTH_REMOTE_ACCESS)   // no tile reach: its remote() ops use REACH_VIEW or REACH_ANY. Authority defaults to AUTH_PHYSICAL
SPECIES_CAPABILITIES(/datum/species/human,                 // excerpt: the human's whole list is in 16.4
	hands(),                                                   // the hand provider: provides(AFF_MANIPULATE | AFF_ATTACK | AFF_HOLD, reach = 1). /mob/living declares none
)
CAPABILITIES(/mob/living/simple_mob/animal/space/carp)   // excerpt: the full list is in 16.7. No species, so no hands: its own provider is its bite
	natural_weapon(/datum/natural_weapon/bite, damage = 10)

Who may act, and what they can reach

Two gates run before any op can match or run. They apply the same way to players, AI controllers and perform_op().

Actor gate. Every actor has a stat acts_via (rule MASK_AND, base ORIGIN_ALL) beside the existing can_act (ALL). Resolution first checks the origin the input arrived on against the actor's acts_via: an origin that is not in the mask drops every candidate. ORIGIN_SYSTEM is not masked. An op with a physical binding (hand(), tool(), item(), stack(), in_hand(), at_target(), inside()) also gets an implicit req_capable(), unless it declares req_capable(ignoring = ...) (struggling ignores restraints).

Conditions hold these stats, so nothing in content checks "is absorbed":

  • absorbed: acts_via = ORIGIN_NONE, held with a reason. Prey cannot click, use a UI, use abilities or speak commands.
  • stunned or paralysed: the stun and paralysis statuses contribute FALSE to can_act (contributes(STAT_CAN_ACT, not(STAT_STUN))).
  • buckled or handcuffed: not an origin matter. The implicit req_capable() on every physical binding refuses with the restraint's reason, and inside() struggling declares req_capable(ignoring = RESTRAINTS), so a restrained prey can still struggle. UI and abilities stay as their sources choose.
  • phase-shifted: acts_via = ORIGIN_VERB | ORIGIN_HOTKEY, abilities only, so the way back still works (16.9).
  • dead: ORIGIN_NONE. A ghost is its own mob, and its ops need AFF_OBSERVE, which only an observer provides.

Ending the condition releases the hold. AUTH_ADMIN bypasses the actor gate, and only with an authority datum.

Reach gate. For an op whose reach policy is REACH_ADJACENT the target must be reachable, and the gate works in four steps, which is what lets a spear reach two tiles:

  1. Collect the candidate providers for the input: the actor's hand, the held item, a selected module, telekinesis.
  2. Distance. The gate takes the largest reach among them and asks whether the target is that near. If the target is out of even the longest reach, no candidate survives.
  3. Exposure. It walks the containment ledger from the target up to the first container the actor can touch, and every slot on the way must be exposed.
  4. Each op then checks its own provider: the provider that gives the affordance the op accepts must itself reach the target. A spear's reach of 2 is therefore known before the gate decides, and an op that needs only a hand still resolves against the hand's reach of 1.

REACH_RANGE(n) and REACH_VIEW skip the two distance steps, 2 and 4. The target must be within the op's own n tiles, or in the actor's view, and a provider's reach neither limits nor extends that (step 1 still names A.provider). The exposure walk, step 3, applies to a target inside a container under every spatial policy.

Spaces and doors

Things behind covers and panels work by one mechanism, a physical path. A holder has nested spaces joined by doors; every slot, op and part that is "inside" names the space it lives in, and reaching it needs a path from the actor's side through open doors. Nothing writes "is the cover open?" by hand.

space(SPACE_X, inside =, door =, applies_to = AUTH_PHYSICAL, latched_while =, because =, from_stage =, missing =)
latch(SPACE_X, condition, because)           // the door can't open while the condition holds
protrudes(SPACE_X, condition, because)       // something in the space keeps the door from closing; inside stage(): while at that stage
at(SPACE_X)                                  // an op, or a whole graph, works inside the space
slot(..., at = SPACE_X, fits = size_is(ITEMSIZE_NORMAL))   // cell_bay(nameof(cell), at = SPACE_CELL, fits = ...)
req_closed(SPACE_X) · req_space_empty(SPACE_X)            // "Close the cover first." · "Remove the power cell first."
  • Spaces. inside names the parent space (null: the holder's outside). door is what closes it: a door capability (CAP_COVER, CAP_PANEL; it answers door_open() and gives the closed reason, "Its cover is closed."), a capability key, or a holder var (nameof(opened) of a locker). applies_to is the authority mask the door stops: AUTH_PHYSICAL, so a hand and telekinesis stop at a closed cover while a remote-access reach (AUTH_REMOTE_ACCESS, an AI's remote()) passes physical doors as before, and reaches only what has a remote() binding. panel() declares its own SPACE_PANEL; maintenance_hatch() declares SPACE_HATCH behind its cover.
  • The path. From the actor's side (its outside, or the space it sits in, a person shut in a locker) out to the common space, then in to the target's space: every space on the way must exist (from_stage) and every door on it be open for the authority. The first one that is not gives the reason. The reach gate's exposure walk (step 3) asks the same path at each container it climbs (space_blocked(): a var slot or a containment-ledger slot placed at a space), and the implicit Require of an op at(SPACE_X) asks it too.
  • Silent or refuse. A candidate whose path is blocked is set aside, not dropped: when another candidate answers the same input it wins silently (an empty hand on a closed APC opens the window instead of reaching for the cell); when none does, the set-aside candidate runs and Require refuses with the blocking door's reason ("Its cover is closed."). A held thing that a set-aside op takes (an item(), tool() or stack() binding: a cell for the bay) is not answered by a later candidate that ignores what is held (a hand() touch, the window), so a cell clicked on a closed APC is refused, not swallowed by the window. Construction steps of a graph placed at(SPACE_X) follow the same rule, so a screwdriver on a shut APC works its panel and on an open one its build step.
  • Latches. latch(SPACE_X, condition, because) (or space(..., latched_while =, because =)): while the condition holds the door can't open. because is a message or a handler that returns one. The APC's cover is latched while it is broken or its cover lock holds a charged cell; maintenance_hatch(panel_needs_cover_closed = TRUE) latches the panel while the cover is open.
  • Protrusions. protrudes(SPACE_X, condition, because): something in the space keeps its door from closing. Written as a part of a stage() it takes no condition, holds while the graph is at that stage and names the graph's space by default: the APC's board, in but not fastened, protrudes from its hatch.
  • A door capability checks its own latches and protrusions: cover() and panel() put req_door_free(CAP_X) on their open ops.
  • Staged spaces. space(SPACE_CELL, inside = SPACE_HATCH, from_stage = STAGE_APC_SECURED, missing = MSG(apc/needs_electronics)): the space exists only from that construction stage on; before it a path into it is refused with missing. A slot in it needs no req_built().
  • Slot acceptance. A slot's fits = is a requirement on the held thing, refused with a generated reason: size_is(min, max) says "That is too large to fit." or "That is too small to fit."
  • Repair is a construction step. A cover's stages are whole and broken (cover(broken = condition): knocked off or the holder not working, by default); replace is the step back to whole, offered only on a broken cover and refused while its space holds something (req_space_empty, "Remove the power cell first.").
  • Library defaults. An electronic lock (lock()) and an emag (emag()) need a holder that works (req_operable()) and nobody subverted (req_not_subverted()), unless configured otherwise (lock(powered = FALSE, guarded = FALSE), emag(powered = FALSE), emag(repeatable = TRUE)).
  • Explain Interaction prints each candidate placed in a space with its path ("path outside -> into hatch (door cover: closed)"), and says whether it was set aside or won blocked, with the door's reason.

Exposure of a slot: SLOT_EXPOSURE_EXTERNAL is always exposed; a slot placed at = SPACE_X is exposed while the path to that space is open for the authority in use.

Reaching the target is Match, so it is silent. An unreachable target is not a candidate, so a closed hatch stops every op aimed at the enclosed cell: its own ops, using a tool on it and drag-drop. Reach applies to the held item's at_target ops too. Examining an unreachable thing shows only what the space exposes.

Gate feedback. Both gates filter silently, so that input can fall through to another candidate. But if no candidate survives and a gate dropped something, the engine shows that gate's reason, rate-limited, so a click never just does nothing. The actor gate's reason comes from the holds on acts_via, which take one: hold(prey, STAT_ACTS_VIA, ORIGIN_NONE, source = src, reason = MSG(vore/absorbed)). When several holds apply, the strongest hold's reason is shown. The reach gate's reason is the blocking door's ("Its cover is closed.").

Resolution

  1. Candidates come from three places: the target's ops (its type table plus every activation on it), the held item's at_target ops, and the actor's own ops (abilities and in_hand self-uses).
  2. Filters, in order. These are the Match stage (section 9), so a candidate that fails one is dropped silently:
    • step 0, actor gate: the origin the input arrived on must be in the actor's acts_via (ORIGIN_SYSTEM is exempt), or every candidate is dropped with the gate's reason;
    • one of the op's bindings accepts that origin, and the authority in use;
    • step 0.5, providers and the reach gate: the candidate providers are collected. For REACH_ADJACENT the largest reach among them decides whether the target is a candidate at all, and then the op's own provider must give an affordance the op accepts within its reach; REACH_RANGE(n) and REACH_VIEW use the op's own range or the actor's view;
    • the op's when conditions hold.
  3. Order: the bind profile's intent list, then the op's tier, then relative priority (priority(above("cover.open")) puts an op just above the named one, whatever the tiers). A bundle that composes ops answering one tool declares their order once, click_order(TOOL_X | BIND_HAND, keys...) (the maintenance hatch: a construction step before the panel or the wires, the subversion reset before the wires, for a hand the construction step, then the wires, then the window), so a type needs no priority(above()) of its own; analyze's op_order lint reports ops one type composes that answer one input at one tier with no order, and counts the hand-written relative priorities against a falling ceiling. Remaining ties go target first, then held item, then actor; then binding specificity; then subtype before parent; then capability declaration order. A target op that names this held item's type (item(T)) is tier PART and the target wins ties, so it outranks the item's own generic use.

    Binding specificity (op_binding_specificity, resolve.dm) breaks a tie between two candidates on the same side that answer one input at one intent and tier, by how precisely each binding names the held thing: an item type narrower than /obj/item first, the deeper type before its ancestor (item(/obj/item/stack/material/plasteel) before item(/obj/item/stack)); then a tool quality (tool(Q) and any_of_tools(...) rank alike); then a broad item (item(/obj/item), stack(/obj/item)). So exact item type > tool quality > broad item type. A broad binding gated by an acceptance proc (the vendor's stock with when(stockable), the airlock's pry_weapon with prying_weapon) still ranks as broad: the proc is opaque, so a screwdriver on a vendor opens the panel before it is stocked. Bindings that name no held item (hand(), in_hand(), at_target(), a dragged item(/mob/living)) are not compared and keep their place: within a run tied on intent, tier and side, only the held-item candidates swap among the slots they already hold (op_specificity_sort). It sits after the side because only target-side bindings name the held item (a held item's at_target() filter names the target), and before declaration order, so a tool op beats a catch-all of the same holder whichever is declared first; the storage catch-all storage.put_in needs no special case. Explicit order wins over it: priority(above/below(...)) and click_order() move an op after the sort. op_order's unordered_clash applies the same rule, so it reports only bindings of equal specificity.

  4. Run: the first candidate runs through Require, Wait and Do. If Require refuses, the player sees the reason; the input never silently falls through. Only Match failures fall through. If no candidate survives and a gate dropped something, the engine shows the gate's reason (see "Gate feedback").
  5. Passing on: an op with a passes() part lets the next candidate run after the first one commits. The later candidates' refusals and Match failures are silent, because the player already got one op's result. explain_click shows the whole chain.
Tier, highest firstYielded by
OP_PRIORITY_SUBVERTThe emag() capability's op: subverting the holder comes before anything else the card could do
OP_PRIORITY_CLAWThe claw swipe: a species or mob op (a natural weapon) that swipes empty-handed at a machine, offered only where a reaction listens for /datum/notice/slashed (req_heard); it publishes the slash action. There is no claws() capability
OP_PRIORITY_TAKE_OUTtake_out(): taking what sits in an open bay or slot by hand, ahead of the holder's own empty-hand use
OP_PRIORITY_ATTACKhostile(), answers(INTENT_ATTACK)
OP_PRIORITY_PARTitem(T), stack(T, n), tool(Q), any_of_tools(), put_in(): a part worked with a named thing in hand (a cover, a panel, wires, a repair)
OP_PRIORITY_NORMALhand(), in_hand(), at_target(), inside(), remote(), menu(), ai(), ui_act(), topic()
OP_PRIORITY_DEFAULTThe interface() open op, pick-up: what a type does when nothing more specific answers

An op's tier is its default place in the order of candidates. It is the highest tier its parts yield, and priority(OP_PRIORITY_X) pins one. Only those two ops, the emag op and the claw swipe, pin the SUBVERT and CLAW tiers; everything else orders by priority(above(...)) and priority(below(...)), which name a key and so are greppable.

The same-input rule. Two ops on one target type with the same input, the same intent and the same tier are a build error naming both lines. The fix is a different input, a different tier or a relative priority, or passes() on the first. Ops whose when parts are mutually exclusive do not clash (when(c) against when(not(c)), or req_is(K) against req_is(K, FALSE)). A belly's "struggle" and "escape" cannot both be plain inside() ops at one tier: one of them gets a different input, such as menu().

Resolution cost

A click, a hover and a menu share one two-pass resolver, so cost stays flat as candidates grow.

  • Pass 1 builds the ordered candidate list from an interned index keyed by intent and held type, applying only the cheap gates: the origin mask, the providers and reach, and the affordance. It runs no requirement code.
  • Pass 2 on a click walks that list testing each candidate's when conditions until one holds. That is the winner, and only its needs() run.
  • Menus and screentips run needs() for every candidate, lazily, with at_most = 0.2 SECONDS and a cache keyed by (actor, target, held, the act generation of each of the three, the actor's provider set generation). The act generation of an entity bumps when a key that one of its requirements reads is published, and requirements read through A.actor, A.held and A.target, so the key names those three entities and carries the generation of each. A generation is a counter per entity, so a key of counters alone would let two actors with equal counters share an entry. The provider set generation is bumped when an activation with provides attaches or detaches, or a held item changes.
  • The reach walk is cached per (actor, the actor's provider set generation, target): it depends on where this actor is, so the key names the actor. The dependency graph invalidates it (section 7): containment and door state (a closing cover is part of reach) are tracked inputs, not hand-written rules. Provider selection, grants and Match conditions are tracked the same way, so a cached menu or a pending wait is invalidated by the graph too. A UI window recomputes the reason of an op only when a key that op's requirements read changed.
  • Bench scenarios gate phase 1 on these paths: 100 clicks per second on a 30-candidate stack, hovering over 60 atoms, and 20 open UI windows (section 19).

Explaining a resolution

Every filter drops candidates silently, so the engine also says why. The tools below use the origin list the table builder keeps for each interned entry (__FILE__:__LINE__, kept in every build, production included).

explain_click(actor, target, held, gesture)         // admin verb "Explain Interaction": every candidate, the filter that dropped it (with the entry's file:line),
                                                    // the winner, its tier and the tie-break that placed it, and any passes() chain
perform_op(actor, target, key, held, trace = TRUE)  // the same path, with the trace printed
explain_type(/obj/machinery/power/apc)              // the merged op table, each part tagged with its origin: type, capability or extend site
assert_resolves(actor, target, held, GESTURE_CLICK, "cover.open")   // a unit-test helper

The first two are admin verbs ("Explain Interaction" and "Explain Type"), and "List Activations" (explain_activations(E), section 5) and "List Pending Ops" print an entity's activations and an actor's pending op, all in production. Keeping the origin lists costs one file and one line per interned entry, which the boot-memory gate measures.

On an empty resolution the engine shows the best near-miss's reason, rate-limited: the highest-ranked candidate dropped by the origin, provider or reach filter, with a message such as "You can't do that without hands." A when miss has no reason by design and stays silent to the player; explain_click names the condition that was false.

Build errors are tagged with the tier that catches them:

TierCatches
DM compileAn unknown ACT_TRY token or wrong field types (the generated act_x() procs are typed); misused part constructors
GeneratorAn unknown read; an unannotated global call inside a requirement, condition or output
Boot (the table builder)An unresolved literal key in extend, without, configure, shares_effects, on_op or priority(above()); a duplicate key; a requirement with no reason; two ops with the same input, intent and tier; a shares_effects() cycle, or a shared op that asks(); an op with consumes() and an item-sourced hold; a cycle among stats and conditions; a handler that reads a field its context type lacks; stacks = BEST(p) naming an unknown param
TestA requirement or condition that writes, publishes or messages (the purity guard, section 9); a runtime cycle refused when its edge was added; a notice queued, or an action refused, past ACT_MAX_DEPTH

Replaces:

  • Handler checks and gates: incapacitated()/can_act checks in handlers, INCAPACITATED_IGNORING, manual "is absorbed" checks, manual "panel open?" guards before reaching contents, hand_gate() (the operability check is extend(TAG_CONTROL, needs(...)) from machine_basics; unbuckle-on-touch is the buckle() capability's op).
  • Entry points: attackby, attack_hand, attack_self, afterattack, attack_generic, *_act tool procs and click_alt overrides; INTERACTION_ENTRY_*; the legacy interaction resolver; cap_use_at's separate pass; use_tool.
  • Results and types: INTERACTION_HANDLED_PASS (now passes()); guard_ctx; op_ctx as a separate type.
  • The ROUTE_* mask and via() (now an origin, a reach policy, a provider and an authority, each its own part), a single acts_via mask of routes (now of origins), a route argument on provides() and compartment() (now authority = and applies_to =), and AFF_INTERFACE and AFF_TK.
  • /datum/ability: an ability is an actor op granted to the actor, and its HUD button is menu(button =).

9Ops and parts

op(key, binding | inputs(bindings...), parts...) // key: a literal id. The menu name is label("Text"), else derived from the key, the tool verbs or toggles(). Nested lists flatten. extend(key | list(keys) | CAP_X | TAG_X | /datum/act/x, parts...) // TAG_X | TAG_Y also works. binds(b...) adds an input, inputs(b...) replaces them without(key)

Every behaviour is a part. A part type declares how its values combine and implements any of the four stage hooks. The engine knows nothing else. Waits, costs, messages and effects are ordinary library parts, written the same way you'd add a new one.

Four stages

An op runs through four engine stages, always in this order.

StageQuestionWhat acts in itOn failure
MatchIs this what the actor is doing?The input bindings, answers, hostile, origin, reach, by, authority, gesture, stance, presents, when(cond...), and the engine's actor gate, provider gate and reach gateSilent. The op is not a candidate, the input falls through to the next candidate, and menus hide it.
RequireIs it allowed now?needs(req...), at(SPACE_X) (the path to the space), the pre-checks of the actions the effects will start, and the require half of costs (costs, cooldown)The reason is shown, menus grey the op out, and the input does not fall through. Re-checked after the wait and right before Do.
WaitHow long does it take?wait(t, keeps =), scaled by the held tool's speed, and every asks() and confirms(), in declaration order: the op's workflow (section 13)Cancelled when a keeps condition breaks, or when a changed read makes a requirement refuse. See "Waiting and asking".
DoWhat happens?chance, effects, committing or releasing, feedback, delayed, quiet. The costs were reserved just before DoA failed chance() plays its else feedback and stops the rest. The first effect that is not OK ends the op (see "Do is a commit").

Costs are reserved between Wait and Do. At the end of Require and Wait, after the last asks() answer, the op's costs are reserved against their resources (foundation X2, below): costs(), consumes() and cooldown() each reserve their amount, and a reservation that cannot be made refuses the op with its reason. Nothing is spent yet. The reservation is also the moment the act takes strong references to its holder, target, held item and actor (Do is a commit).

Do runs in this order:

  1. Chance: chance(p, else = parts) rolls, after the last answer and after the reservation. A failed roll plays its else feedback, commits the reserved costs (the attempt cost them), runs no effect, and ends the op ACT_COMMITTED with A.rolled = FALSE. It publishes no op_done notice unless a listener opted in: on_op(key, ..., outcome = ACT_ROLL_FAILED) hears the failed rolls, and outcome = ACT_ANY hears every outcome (committed, refused, replaced and failed rolls). The filter is the one on_notice uses (section 8).
  2. Effects: then, toggles, sets, holds, releases, toggles_hold, grants, put_in, take_out, fixes, becomes, spawns, opens_ui, shares_effects, passes, cuts_all_wires, in declaration order. They never sleep, and each reports OP_OK, OP_REFUSED, OP_REPLACED or OP_FAILED (below).
  3. Commit or release: if every effect reported OP_OK the reservations commit: the costs are spent, a consumed item is deleted, the cooldown starts. Otherwise the unspent reservations are released.
  4. Feedback and notice, only if committed with a successful roll: says, plays, flash, logs, fingerprints, and the op_done notice.
  5. Later and quiet: delayed(t, parts...) schedules more parts on the holder's clock, owned by the holder and dying with it, and quiet() suppresses the notice.

asks() runs in Wait. It is a step of the op's workflow (section 13), not a part of the effect list. Within Wait the steps run in declaration order: the timed wait() and each asks() and confirms() run where the op writes them, so "confirm, then work" is confirms(...), wait(...) and "work, then ask" the other way round (a wait that no declaration places, from a tool profile or an extend() on an op that wrote none, runs first). The reservation follows the last step, so cancelling a prompt costs nothing. An effect declared before an asks() would run before the prompt and before costs are reserved, so the build rejects it: nothing irreversible happens before the last answer, and chance() rolls after it. The answer arrives as A.answer; with several prompts it is the latest, and each is also kept under its step name (A.step("rename")). After each answer, and after a timed wait that follows one, the op's when and Require parts are checked again, and the fields the op declared are restored from the capture it took at its first asks() or confirms() (section 13).

Metadata, not stages. The label() name, priority() and tag() say how an op is listed and ordered. They never run.

Combine rules stay per part. Requirements accumulate. Single values (input, wait, cooldown, priority, says, plays, verbs, label) replace, and the most specific declaration wins. Effects append and tags accumulate. Costs combine per resource: the most specific costs(RES_X, n) replaces, and costs(RES_X, +n) adds to it.

Do is a commit

Effects can start refusable world actions: put_in() runs the insert action, and a hook may refuse or take it over. Do is therefore a commit with five rules.

  1. Checks before irreversible work. Require runs first, then the pre-checks of the domain actions the effects will start: their needs are evaluated as requirements, with reasons, before any cost is reserved. A custom effect that starts an action declares it, then(PROC_REF(x), checks = /datum/act/insert); the library effect parts declare theirs (put_in() and take_out() the insert and remove actions, consumes() the consuming of the held item, costs() its resource). A pre-check that refuses ends the op refused, with its reason, having spent nothing.
  2. Reserve, commit, release. Resources are reserved at the end of Require and Wait, after the last answer, committed after the effects succeed, and released on refusal or failure (X2, below).
  3. Effects report. Each effect reports one of four defines: OP_OK, OP_REFUSED, OP_REPLACED or OP_FAILED. A then() proc that returns nothing reports OP_OK; any other return value must be an OP_* constant, which a lint checks; a runtime is OP_FAILED. The library parts report for themselves: put_in() reports OP_REFUSED when the insert is refused and OP_REPLACED when a takeover stops it, and grants() reports OP_FAILED when the grant returns null. A custom effect that starts a world action returns act_outcome_to_op(F) for it (committed is OP_OK, refused OP_REFUSED, replaced OP_REPLACED), so a refusal or takeover reaches the op. A compound mutation that must be atomic is one engine verb (slot_transfer, replace_with, consume), never two effects.
  4. The outcome is the first effect that is not OK, else committed. Only a committed op whose roll succeeded publishes its op_done notice (on_op), its success messages and its fingerprints: a failed chance() is committed, because its costs were spent, but A.rolled is FALSE and none of those happen unless a listener opted in (above). A refused, replaced or failed op releases its unspent reservations, keeps the effects it already applied (there is no general rollback), logs its outcome with its key chain, and gives the actor the refusal reason. The outcome is an action outcome (section 8): committed is ACT_COMMITTED, refused is ACT_REFUSED, replaced is ACT_REPLACED, and a failed op is reported as ACT_REFUSED with the runtime logged.
  5. Weak until the reservation, strong from it. While the op waits, which includes every asks(), the act holds its holder, target, held item and actor through weak relations, and deleting one cancels the op (ACT_REFUSED, with the reason). From the reservation until the act finishes it holds all four strongly. Consuming or replacing them (consumes(ON_HELD), replace_with) never cancels the op that did it, and later feedback reads the act's snapshot fields (A.held_name, A.target_name, A.actor_name), never a deleted object.

When or needs

when() takes conditions and needs() takes requirements (below). They differ in what a failure means. A failed when(cond...) is a Match failure: the op is not what the actor is doing, so it is silently not a candidate and the input falls through. A failed needs(req...) is a Require failure: the actor is refused, with a reason.

when means "exists only while". It has two spellings with one meaning. On one op it is a part: op("eject", hand(), when(nameof(beaker)), then(PROC_REF(eject))). Around several ops it is the entry-level wrapper when(cond, op(...), op(...)), the same when as in section 5. Both compile to the same resolution-time applicability check. Nothing is added to or removed from the op table at runtime.

Rule of thumb. If the op shouldn't exist in that state, so it should be hidden, use when. If a player would ask "why can't I?", use needs.

Conditions and requirements

A condition is a boolean with no reason. A requirement is a condition plus a reason. The grammar of a condition is the same everywhere one is taken: when(), contributes(), every(when =), outputs(when =), look_layer(when =), on_change(cond, ...) and sequence steps.

Condition formMeaning
nameof(v)Truthy test of a tracked var or relation on the holder
STAT_X, or a capability key id (COVER_OPEN)Truthy test of that stat or key
not(c) · all_of(c...) · any_of(c...)Negation, and, or
PROC_REF(x) · CAP_PROC(x)x(datum/act/A) returns TRUE or FALSE
A library requirement datumIts boolean view (req_built_final() as a condition)

Strings are never conditions, and an expression such as !operable && integrity_broken is not one: write a PROC_REF.

When a condition is evaluated. A stat recomputes inline whenever its inputs change (on the written entity and one single-valued edge away), so it is settled before the writer's next line, and a condition that gates a stat contribution is one of those inputs. A condition used for matching (when() on an op, menus, look_layer(when =)) is evaluated when it is read, at resolution or output time. A condition that gates hooks, observes or outputs (an entry-level when() around them, every(when =) starting and stopping, on_change edges, while_slotted hooks) is evaluated at the drain (section 7). An op under when() is read at resolution. A condition inside an op's Match or Require runs in that op's context, so it can read the actor, the held item and the target; anywhere else it gets the evaluation context.

needs() takes requirements only. Every requirement carries a resolvable reason, or the build fails:

  • Library constructors carry a default reason from MSG_DEF(req/<ctor>, ...). Capability keys declared with cap_keys() carry declared reasons, so needs(req_is(COVER_OPEN)) needs no because =. A declared reason is the message for the test the capability usually makes; testing a key the other way, or one with no declared reason, takes an explicit because =.
  • Generic tests take an explicit reason: req_is(KEY, value = TRUE, because = MSG(x)), req_at_least(KEY, n, because = MSG(x)) and req(PROC_REF(cond), because = MSG(x)). KEY is nameof(v), a STAT_X id or a capability key id.
  • A dynamic reason: because = PROC_REF(reason_proc), where reason_proc(datum/act/A) returns a /datum/msg type.
  • Combining: not(r, because = MSG(x)) needs its because inside needs(). any_of(...) joins its reasons ("needs a wrench or a welder"). all_of(...) reports the first one that fails.
  • Relaxing: a requirement may take id = "x", so a subtype can drop that one deliberately: extend("cover.open", drop = "x").

The old REQ_ON, REQ_TARGET_STATE and REQ_PROC become req(PROC_REF(x), because = ...) once their procs take (datum/act/A) and return a boolean; the mapping says so (section 17).

Purity. Requirements, conditions and because procs must not write state, publish or message. Test builds run them under the same no-write, no-publish, no-message guard as outputs, which is what lets menus, examine and early cancels ask at any time.

Reads. What a requirement reads is generated (section 7). Reads through A.actor, A.held and A.target are typed hops, subscribed for as long as an op is pending, so a wait notices the actor's hands or the held item's charge changing. A.args, world.time and GLOB reads do not subscribe. A global helper is followed through READS_FROM(arg), and an unannotated global call inside a requirement, condition or output is a build error with that hint.

GroupLibrary requirements
What is usedreq(T, of = ON_HELD) (the participant is of type T; a type path as the first argument) · req_empty_hand() · req_self_held() · carried() (the item is held, worn or in a container the actor carries: the old REQ_IN_INVENTORY, which is wider than in_hand()) · req_component(T) (a machine has that component installed)
Target statereq_tag(TAG) · req_property_at_most(PROP, n, of = ON_TARGET) (a property such as a size class is at most n) · req_wire(W) (the wire is intact) · req_wire_cut(W) · req_built(STAGE_X) · req_built_final() · req_full(nameof(rel)) · req_empty(nameof(rel)) · req_not_subverted()
Actorreq_conscious() · req_alive() · req_capable(ignoring =) · req_fits_bodytype() (the item fits the wearer's body type; used on equips) · req_adjacent() · req_stance(S) · req_access() · req_rights(R_X) · req_heard(/datum/notice/x) (something on the target listens for that notice, so an op that only matters when a reaction exists is offered only then)

The one signature

Every proc the engine calls to run a part or a trigger takes (datum/act/A); the exceptions are the interfaces named in ground rule 7. PROC_REF(x) runs on the holder (src is the holder). CAP_PROC(x) runs on the capability datum, with A.holder the entity and A.cap the datum. The table lists, per place, which fields are set.
