Keys, tags and UI ops

Keys. Every key is a declared identifier that the build resolves. An unresolved one is a build error, and keys of different kinds are never interchangeable.

KindWritten asRules
Op keyA literal: "breaker" for a type's own op, "cover.open" for an op a capability bringsA capability's ops are namespaced by it automatically, and by its selector when it has one ("cell_bay.cell.insert", section 11). A bare key has no dot. Keys are unique in a type's compiled table; a duplicate is a build error naming both lines.
Entry keykey = "scan" on any other entry (every, on_notice, on_change, contributes, ...)Shares the op-key namespace on a type, so one extend, without or configure can address either
Capability keyCAP_X plus a selector (section 11)An identical repeat dedupes. The same key with different params is a build error naming both lines
TagTAG_XTags accumulate. Every ui_act() op gets TAG_UI and every topic() op gets TAG_TOPIC, automatically. An extend on a tag that matches nothing is fine.

The build resolves every literal reference in extend, without, configure, shares_effects, perform_op, on_op and priority(above()) against the type's compiled table. perform_op with an unknown key at runtime is an error, not a no-op. Stats, sources and build stages have their own ids (STAT_X, SRC_X, STAGE_X), and their own sections.

UI ops. ui_act() with no name uses the op's key as the window action's name; ui_act("legacy") is only for a TSX interface that cannot change. Invalid or missing args reject the press (or clamp a number, per the schema) and log it; the handler never runs on an invalid value. Whether the actor "can use the window" (range, conscious, rights) is checked as a requirement before the op's own. Sub-actions use dotted names, ui_act("tank.eject"), which replace UI_SUBACT. An op can have several bindings, so a window button and a click are one op with two inputs, not two ops (section 8).

Intents. The input part implies a default intent, and select parts override it (the input table in section 8).

Extend targets, overrides and precedence

extend takes one of: "key" (an op or entry key), list(keys), CAP_X (all of that capability's ops), TAG_X (or TAG_X | TAG_Y: ops with either tag), or /datum/act/x (hook parts: needs, instead, adjusts).

Precedence, lowest to highest: library defaults, then the op's own parts, then group extends (CAP, TAG, action; same level, in list order), then extend("key"). A subtype beats its parent at each level. Requirements accumulate, single values replace, effects append and tags accumulate. Capabilities granted at runtime form a second table with the same precedence, resolved at click time; extend(CAP_X, ...) is by key, so it applies to one granted later.

Extending a key that doesn't exist is a build error. The one exception is an inherited extend whose target a later without() removed: it is dropped quietly.

A subtype wants toWrite
Add a requirementextend("pry", needs(...)), or on CAP_X, TAG_X or an action
Relax one inherited requirementThe requirement is declared with id = "x"; extend("cover.open", drop = "x")
Swap an op's effectsextend("pry", replaces(then(PROC_REF(do_open))))
Retune an entry's named argsconfigure("scan", interval = 10 SECONDS) for an entry with a key; configure(x(param = v)) for a capability: its params are the constructor's named args, the entries are rebuilt, and variant = subtype swaps the datum type. An unknown key or param is a build error
Remove something inheritedwithout(key): an op, an entry or a capability, applied at its position in the list

Resource transactions (X2)

RESOURCE_DEF(RES_X, adapter = /datum/resource/x, schema =) /datum/resource/x/proc/holder_of(datum/act/A) // the entity whose store holds the amount (default: the actor); open reservations are totalled per holder /datum/resource/x/proc/available(datum/act/A) // how much there is: the raw amount, before reservations /datum/resource/x/proc/reserve(datum/act/A, n) // sets n aside and returns a /datum/reservation, or null when there is not enough /datum/resource/x/proc/commit(datum/reservation/R) // spends the reserved amount: returns OP_OK (a commit that returns nothing is OP_OK), or OP_FAILED when it cannot /datum/resource/x/proc/release(datum/reservation/R) // gives it back /datum/reservation(resource, holder, amount, op) // the record: R.resource, R.holder, R.amount, R.op (the op's act) res_spend(E, RES_X, n, actor = E, held = null, target = null) // code outside an op: reserve and commit in one call, with an explicit context; returns the amount spent: n, or 0 when the reservation was refused (reserve is all or nothing)

A resource is something an op takes in discrete amounts. Its adapter knows where the amount lives (the held tool, the actor, the target, a slot) and answers five calls. A reservation is a /datum/reservation(resource, holder, amount, op): holder is what holder_of(A) named. The base adapter implements reserve and release over available, keeping the open reservations in the holder's own lazy reservations list (declared LIST_STATE(/datum, reservations) in code/base, so the instance-list lint accepts it), so deleting the holder drops them, and what it may set aside is available less that total. Most adapters write only available and commit; one that must touch a second entity overrides the rest. A reservation expires with the op: however the op ends, the engine commits or releases every reservation it holds, so nothing is lost and nothing leaks. A commit() that fails reports OP_FAILED with a log line naming the resource and holder, the outcome follows rule 4 of Do is a commit, and the unspent reservations are released. costs(RES_X, n) is the op part: its Require half is available, it reserves after the last answer, and it commits after the effects.

res_spend() takes the context an adapter reads explicitly: a fuel or stack adapter that reads A.held gets held =, and a slot adapter gets target =. An amount passes the resource's schema like any internal write, so an int resource rounds a fraction (a reservation rounds up).

ResourceTaken from
RES_FUELThe held tool's fuel (a welder, a jetpack)
RES_USESThe held item's use count
RES_CHARGEThe held item's cell charge, per use
RES_STACKUnits of the held stack. reserve sets the units aside. Under a stack(T, n) binding with put_in(), the put splits off exactly those units and moves the split, and the commit consumes nothing further, because the split already moved them (16.14); without a put_in() the commit deletes the units
RES_ITEMThe item consumes() takes: the held item, or the one item(T) matched. reserve claims it, so no other op can use it meanwhile; commit deletes it; release lets it go
RES_COOLDOWNAn op's cooldown(t). reserve checks that the cooldown is ready and claims it; commit starts it; release leaves it ready
RES_BLOODThe actor's blood volume (a surgical or a vampiric op)
RES_DARK_ENERGYThe actor's dark energy (phase shift, 16.9)
RES_REAGENTSA reagent holder's volume. A transfer is a reservation on the source and a reservation of capacity on the target, committed together, so a full target refuses before anything leaves the source (16.5)
RES_SLOT_CAPACITYA slot's or a storage's free capacity: an insert reserves it, and the move commits it (section 6)
The state-graph ledgerEach edge's ledger entry is written when its costs are reserved and finalised at commit; undoing the edge refunds that entry (section 12)

The boundary. Only discrete spends are transactions. Continuous draw, the power_draw stat, a heater's load, a per-tick drain applied while an effect lasts, stays a stat or per-tick work feeding its domain: there is nothing to reserve and nothing to refund. A per-tick drain that takes a resource in steps calls res_spend() each step.

Tool profiles and bundles

TOOL_PROFILE(TOOL_MULTITOOL,   wait(5 SECONDS), plays(SFX_MULTITOOL),   verbs("pulse", "pulses"))
TOOL_PROFILE(TOOL_SCREWDRIVER, wait(2 SECONDS), plays(SFX_SCREWDRIVER), verbs("screw", "unscrew"))
TOOL_PROFILE(TOOL_WELDER,      wait(3 SECONDS), plays(SFX_WELDER),      verbs("weld", "unweld"), costs(RES_FUEL, 1))
// tool(Q) expands to its profile as defaults per slot: the wait, the sound, the verbs and one costs() per resource.
// An op's own wait, plays, verbs or costs(RES_X, n) replaces that slot. Write costs(RES_FUEL, +1) to add one.
// A profile's wait() is the base the held tool's speed scales: tool_quality(Q, speed = 1.5) makes it shorter.
// any_of_tools(Q...) uses the profile of the tool actually in hand. The affordance a tool(Q) binding needs is AFF_TOOL_<Q>,
// which any item with tool_quality(Q) provides, so a borg module that declares it can do the tool's ops.
// Your own bundles are procs returning parts, and nest freely. A bundle is a global proc, so a holder proc it names
// is TYPE_PROC_REF(/type, x), never PROC_REF(x):
/proc/force_pry()
	return list(tool(TOOL_CROWBAR), hostile())
/proc/component_swap(item_type, delay = 5 SECONDS)
	return list(item(item_type), consumes(), wait(delay), fixes())

Holds, grants and sources

holds(), releases() and grants() place stat contributions and capabilities through the same store as hold() and grant() (section 5). Their source defaults by where the effect lands. On another entity (a baton's stun on the mob it hits, a pill's effect on whoever eats it) it is the op source: the pair of holder and op key, owned by the target's store, so the effect survives the item that carried the op being used up, and it ends by releases(), by expiry or when the target goes. On the op's own holder it is the op's activation (X1), so it ends with the capability: a phase shift's phased ends when a species change drops the shift (16.9). A timed hold on another entity runs its course even if its source is deleted first.

  • outlives = TRUE on a holds() or grants() that lands on the op's own holder makes it independent of the activation: its source is the op source, and it runs its course.
  • source = SRC_X names a SOURCE_DEF flyweight (SRC_AI_CONTROL).
  • source = SRC_HELD_ITEM makes the held item own it: it lasts while the item is in that hand or slot, and ends if the item is deleted. An op with consumes() and an item-sourced hold or grant is a build error.
  • source = ON_ACTOR makes the actor the source: one AI's bolts never release another's. In a handler's code the same is hold(E, STAT_X, v, source = A.actor).
  • bound = TRUE, as on hold(), makes a timed hold die with its source instead of running its course. It is independent of outlives: outlives chooses the source (the activation or the op source), and bound whether a timed hold dies with that source. A hold the activation owns is bound unless outlives = TRUE.

Waiting and asking

wait(t, keeps = WAIT_KEEPS_DEFAULT)            // keeps: HELD | ADJACENT | TARGET_PRESENT | ALIVE
op("pry", label("Pry open"), force_pry(), wait(5 SECONDS, keeps = HELD | ADJACENT), then(PROC_REF(do_open)))
op("rename", label("Rename"), in_hand(), asks(/datum/prompt/text/rename), then(PROC_REF(apply_name)))
/obj/item/proc/apply_name(datum/act/A)
	var/datum/prompt/text/rename/R = A.answer       // typed: the answered prompt
	name = R.value
KeepThe wait is cancelled when
HELDthe held item is dropped, swapped or deleted
ADJACENTthe actor is no longer adjacent to the target (a binding with a spatial reach only)
TARGET_PRESENTthe target moved away (its deletion cancels the op whatever the keeps say, below)
ALIVEthe actor dies or loses consciousness
  • Default: WAIT_KEEPS_DEFAULT is all four, where they apply: no HELD without a held item, no ADJACENT for a binding with no spatial reach. keeps = HELD narrows it.
  • A changed read re-runs the requirements. The wait is cancelled only if one now refuses, so a cell's charge ticking down does not interrupt a wait that is still allowed.
  • The pending op is a kernel record related to the actor, the target and the held item through weak relations. Deleting any of them before the reservation cancels it, with feedback; from the reservation the act holds them strongly (Do is a commit). It subscribes to the reads of the requirements it will re-check, and drops them when it ends. VV and explain_click list an actor's pending op and its subscriptions.
  • An actor has any number of pending ops. Waiting on an answer is never exclusive: any number of asks() and confirms() questions, each its own window bound to its own op, are open at once, up to OP_PENDING_CAP (10) per actor; one more is refused with a message, and nothing is cancelled silently.
  • Exclusivity is claims(mask). CLAIM_HANDS and CLAIM_BODY are the actor's, CLAIM_TARGET is an exclusive thing (claims() alone is all three, claims(0) none). Ops whose claims overlap conflict; ops that share none coexist. A timed wait() on a physical binding that declares no claims() holds hands and body while the wait runs; a question holds nothing. The target claim lasts for the whole pending op; a second claiming op on it is refused (/datum/msg/op/claimed).
  • A second input that needs what a waiting op holds: the actor's policy decides. BUSY_REPLACE for players: the older op is cancelled with "You stop what you were doing." and the new one starts. BUSY_REFUSE for AI and scripted actors, so perform_op() during a physical wait is refused. Any physical input needs the hands.
  • The same op on the same target while its question is open shows that window again (it comes to the front) instead of opening a second.
  • asks() takes the same keeps =, with the same default, so reach while waiting is re-checked as part of keeps: an op with a spatial binding is cancelled when the actor walks out of range (ADJACENT) or drops the held item (HELD) during a prompt, as it would during a timed wait.
  • An open asks() holds no actor claim and has its own timeout. Its prompt closes by itself, with the reason, the moment the op stops being possible: the subscription is per pending op, so a requirement that now refuses closes that window and no other (section 13).
  • A resumed op reads what it saw. An op that waits on a request declares per field what it reads after resuming, resume = CAPTURE | LATEST | CANCEL_IF_CHANGED, and the default is CAPTURE: the value the player confirmed is the value the op uses (section 13). The fields that count are the ones a field arg of an asks() names (nameof(v)) and the ones a captures(nameof(v), ...) part names, so a confirms() op captures exactly what it lists. The op's handlers read a captured field with A.captured(nameof(v)), and a lint enforces it for both kinds: a then() handler of an op that has captured fields and reads one directly (src.selected_id) is an error, because DM has no getter to redirect the read and it would see the live value.
  • shares_effects(key) runs another op's Require and effects in this op's act, with no Match, no Wait and none of its costs, and the other op may not asks(). A shares_effects cycle is a build error. It is the explicit, rare form of sharing; to give an existing op a second input, add a binding (extend(key, binds(ui_act()))), which runs the whole op.

Interruption and flows

  • An op paused at an asks() or confirms() holds what it holds while it waits (actor, target, held item, reach, and what its requirements read: a machine's power or operability). If any of it is lost while the prompt is open, the op is cancelled, the prompt closes, and the actor gets the same feedback a cancelled wait() gives (on_interrupt() runs as for a wait). A deleted actor, target or held item cancels it whatever the keeps say.
  • An answer re-runs the requirements before the op goes on. A requirement that now refuses ends the op with that requirement's reason; nothing is spent (costs are reserved after the last answer).
  • A multi-step flow is a chain of asks() steps in one op, each step's answer kept in the op (A.step_value("name"), when = skips a step). There is no flow type. The same cancellation applies between steps.
  • keeps = on asks() is the opt-in for the rare prompt that outlives the actor-side keeps (keeps = 0: the actor may walk away or drop the item and still answer). Requirements are re-run on the answer regardless. The default also drops STAY: an open question outlives a step the actor takes unless the binding has a spatial reach.
  • Pinned by dq_prompt_interrupt/*.

A new kind of part

// A part type declares how it combines and implements any of the stage hooks:
//   match(A)        Match: TRUE if the op matches (silent on failure)
//   require(A)      Require: TRUE if allowed; reason(A) returns the /datum/msg shown when it isn't
//   wait_time(A)    Wait: how long
//   do(A)           Do: the work
// Reads through A.actor, A.held and A.target are typed hops, so the pending op re-checks when they change.
/datum/part/both_hands
	combine = PART_ALL
/datum/part/both_hands/require(datum/act/A)
	return A.actor.hands_free() >= 2
/datum/part/both_hands/reason(datum/act/A)
	return /datum/msg/req_two_hands

// A resource a part can spend is an adapter, not code in the part (X2). The base adapter reserves and releases
// over available(), so this one writes only where the amount lives and how it is spent.
RESOURCE_DEF(RES_BLOOD, adapter = /datum/resource/blood, schema = num(0))
/datum/resource/blood/proc/available(datum/act/A)
	return A.actor.blood_volume
/datum/resource/blood/proc/commit(datum/reservation/R)
	R.holder.use_blood(R.amount)

// An op that spends it:  op("transfuse", hand(), costs(RES_BLOOD, 50), then(PROC_REF(give_blood)))

The pipeline, every time

  1. Match. The actor gate, the provider and reach gates, then the input binding, intent, origin, authority, affordance, stance and every when part. It runs when the action is resolved. A failure is silent: the op is not a candidate, the input falls through, and menus hide it.
  2. Require. Every needs, at and the require half of costs. These run when the menu is built (a failing op is greyed with its reason), when the op is chosen, after the wait and right before Do. A failure shows the reason, and the input does not fall through.
  3. Wait. wait(t, keeps =), scaled by the held tool's speed, and every asks() and confirms(), in declaration order. It is cancelled when a keeps condition breaks or a requirement now refuses. An actor may have many pending ops; only overlapping claims() conflict, and an open prompt claims nothing. After each answer the when and Require parts are checked again and the captured fields are restored. Costs are reserved after the last step.
  4. Do. chance() rolls (a failure plays its else feedback, commits the costs, publishes no notice by default and stops here), effects run in order, never sleep, and each reports OP_OK, OP_REFUSED, OP_REPLACED or OP_FAILED. If all were OK the reservations commit, otherwise they are released; a committed op's feedback plays and its delayed() work is scheduled.

Then, only if the op committed with a successful roll and unless it is quiet(): fingerprints are added, the log line is written, the holder is marked changed, and /datum/notice/op_done is published to whoever wants it (on_op), carrying the op's outcome.

Replaces:

  • Constructors: cap_op and its 35 keywords, cap_hand/cap_tool/cap_use_on/cap_insert/cap_control/cap_use_self/cap_use_at, lib_op, adopt_entry, op_attach.
  • Changing ops: refine(), cap_require(), replace().
  • Interactions: every INTERACT_* spec, DECLARE_INTERACTIONS/EXTEND_INTERACTIONS, dq_interaction_from_spec.
  • Requirement languages: the REQ_*/PRED_*/REQ_ON/REQ_BECAUSE/HOLD_* macros, REQ_FIELD*/REQ_PANEL/REQ_TARGET_STATE, /datum/predicate (outside slots).
  • Old gating and UI forms: else_say, works_broken/works_unpowered, behind/blocked_by/locked_by, UI_ACT/UI_ARG_*/UI_ACT_*/UI_SUBACT (now dotted names), TOPIC_ACTION/TOPIC_* args.
  • Housekeeping now done by the pipeline: add_fingerprint in handlers, manual admin logging in handlers.
  • performs(key) as the way to give an op another input (now a binding; the explicit shares_effects(key) remains for sharing effects only), a single input per op, and arg_num, arg_int, arg_text, arg_bool, arg_choice, arg_ref, arg_path and arg_list (now arg(name, schema)).
  • Spend-then-effect sequences written in handlers (take fuel, charge, a stack or blood, then act, then refund by hand on a refusal): now reservations that commit or release (X2).

10Hooks and events

Hooks come in two forms. A before hook is an extend on an action: it can refuse the action, take it over, or modify it. An after hook (on_notice, on_op, on_change) reacts once something has happened. Nothing else is a hook.

You wantWriteContract
To refuse an action or opextend(/datum/act/x | "op.key" | CAP_X | TAG_X, needs(req...))Pure. A condition plus a reason; every one is checked, and the first refusal in order is shown.
To take an action overextend(/datum/act/x, instead(parts..., order = ORDER_EARLY | ORDER_NORMAL | ORDER_LATE))Side effects allowed. The first taker in order ends the action (spontaneous vore, a shield blocking a hit, a mirror reflecting a projectile). chance() inside means "takes over with probability p"; when(cond) inside gates it. The action ends ACT_REPLACED, and its past-tense notice goes only to listeners that asked for replaced actions.
To modify an action in flightextend(/datum/act/x, adjusts(field, by =, scale =, when =))Changes one typed field of the action before it reaches its sink (armour, ablative shields). Never re-enters the action.
To react after something happenedon_notice(/datum/notice/x, parts..., outcome =)Synchronous at act_done(), in publish order, never coalesced, never sleeps. For committed actions unless outcome = asks for more (section 8).
To react to one op finishingon_op("cover.open", parts...)Sugar for on_notice(/datum/notice/op_done, parts..., op = "cover.open"), which filters on the op key, with the same outcome = vocabulary (ACT_COMMITTED by default). The filter runs before anything is allocated.
To react to a stateon_change(cond, ENTER | EXIT, parts...) or on_change(key, ANY, parts...)An edge trigger on a condition, or a change of a key's value. The condition or key is evaluated at the drain, and the hook runs there (section 7). Replaces power_change() overrides.
An effect while something is in a slotwhile_slotted(SLOT_X, entries..., on = ON_CONTENTS | ON_HOLDER)An activation (X1, section 5) scoped to the item's time in the slot. Its source is the occupying item for ON_HOLDER and the slot entry for ON_CONTENTS.
To announce what can't be refusedACTION(x, ..., FIXED), then PUBLISH(E, x, field = v)Allocates nothing when nobody listens.
World-wide occurrences (round start, shift change, alerts)PUBLISH(world_owner(), x, ...); systems declare on_noticeSame.
Another entity listens at runtimeobserve(source, /datum/notice/x, listener, parts...) / unobserve(source, /datum/notice/x, listener)An activation (X1) of a hook capability on source, sourced by the listener. Either end dying ends it.

Hooks on an action

// Refuse: this slot takes only cells. Pure; the reason is shown.
extend(/datum/act/insert, needs(req(PROC_REF(takes_only_cells), because = MSG(slot/cells_only))))

// Take over: a blob hit rips the panel open and the hit ends here.
extend(/datum/act/hit/blob, instead(cuts_all_wires(), sets(PANEL_OPEN, TRUE), order = ORDER_EARLY))

// Take over with a gate and a chance: a mirror reflects some projectiles.
extend(/datum/act/hit/projectile, instead(when(PROC_REF(mirror_ready)), chance(30), then(PROC_REF(reflect))))

// Modify: ablative armour takes a third off, with no re-entry. field is a typed field of the action.
extend(/datum/act/hit/projectile, adjusts(packet.amount, scale = 0.67, when = PROC_REF(shield_up)))
  • Candidates. The hooks and ops that apply to an action are the target's type table plus every activation on it (grants, while_slotted, species, traits), with the same precedence as ops. A mirror worn on the wearer is a granted capability, so its hooks reach hits on the wearer. Where several activations of one capability are present, its stacking policy (STACK, UNIQUE or BEST(param), section 11) decides which of them run.
  • Order. order = ORDER_EARLY | ORDER_NORMAL | ORDER_LATE, then runs_after = CAP_X, then declaration order. It matters for instead, where the first taker wins and later ones never run, and for adjusts, which all apply in order, each doing its scale then its by. needs hooks do not depend on it beyond which refusal is shown.
  • Takeover. A failed chance() or a false when inside instead means this hook does not take over; the next candidate is tried, and if none does the action proceeds. A hook that takes over may start new actions (a reflected projectile is a new hit), counted against the depth cap in section 8.
  • adjusts fields are named by the action's declared fields (packet.amount for a hit) and checked at build. The caller then executes with the act's final fields, not its own locals (ACT_FINAL, section 8). Use it for armour, shields and resistances; use instead only when the action should not happen.
  • Conditions in hooks. A when() inside instead() and the when = of adjusts() receive the action's own typed context (a /datum/act/hit/projectile for a projectile hit), not the evaluation context, so they can read its fields.
  • Ops are actions too. extend(/datum/act/op, needs(...)) reaches every op on the target, and extend("cover.open", needs(...)) one of them. The target's op hooks apply to ops a capability brought.

After hooks

on_notice(/datum/notice/hit/melee, then(PROC_REF(on_hit)))
on_op("cover.open", then(PROC_REF(cover_opened)))
on_change(PROC_REF(is_powered), ENTER | EXIT, then(PROC_REF(power_flipped)))   // replaces a power_change() override
on_change(nameof(terminal.charge), ANY, then(PROC_REF(charge_changed)))          // a numeric key, through a relation hop: ANY only
on_notice(/datum/notice/hit/projectile, then(PROC_REF(deflected)), outcome = ACT_REPLACED)   // opt in: hear the hits a takeover stopped
  • Hook parts. The parts inside a hook are the Do parts of section 9 (then, sets, toggles, holds, says, ...). A hook has no input, Match or Wait. Its handler takes (datum/act/A), with A.holder set.
  • Two shapes. on_change(cond, ENTER | EXIT, ...) takes a condition (section 9, a boolean, including a hop through a relation): ENTER fires when it becomes true, EXIT when it becomes false. on_change(key, ANY, ...) takes a key (a tracked var or stat, through a relation hop if needed) and fires when its value changed. A numeric key is allowed only with ANY; ENTER or EXIT on one is a build error, because a number is not a condition. A key with no edge argument means ANY. It runs once per drain.
  • Coalescing. coalesce(interval) is a part of on_notice() and on_change(): parts before it gate each trigger, parts after it run once per window. Triggers inside a window are absorbed, so a perception hook that fires for every chunk change and every hurt member runs once per second. The context of the run carries A.holder and A.activation but not the fields of the trigger (a pooled notice is gone by then): read the state that changed. A window is cancelled with its holder or its activation: the pending run of a revoked capability never happens. Rules: section 3.
  • Notices are named in the past tense and generated by ACTION() (section 8). A notice is delivered at act_done(), to a listener on the target's type table or an activation on it, and by default only for a committed action: outcome = ACT_REPLACED or ACT_ANY opts in to the others.

While something is in a slot

CAPABILITIES(/obj/machinery/stasis)   // excerpt: the full list is in 16.4
	buckle(slots = 1)
	while_slotted(SLOT_BUCKLE, holds(STAT_CLOCK_RATE_BIO, 0.1), on = ON_CONTENTS)   // the occupant, while buckled in
CAPABILITIES(/obj/item/shield/mirror)
	while_slotted(SLOT_ANY_WORN, blocks(25), reflects(PROJECTILES, 30), on = ON_HOLDER)   // the wearer, while it is worn
  • on = ON_CONTENTS, declared on the type that owns the slot, grants the entries to what is in the slot: a stasis bed's occupant, a belly's prey (absorption), cryo, a borg dock.
  • on = ON_HOLDER, declared on the item, grants them to the entity whose slot the item sits in: the wearer of a shield or mirror.
  • The entries are the usual ones: holds(STAT_X, v), contributes(...), a capability constructor, blocks(chance), reflects(kinds, chance). The slot entry is an activation scoped to the item's time in the slot (X1): contributions apply at once, hooks and ops attach at the drain, and all of it ends by the one teardown path, however the item leaves. No code releases anything.
  • A hook granted with on = ON_HOLDER sees the granting item as A.source (the vest, the mirror); one granted with on = ON_CONTENTS sees the slot entry.

Announcing and observing

ACTION(round_started, FIXED)                      // declared once; the notice is /datum/notice/round_started
PUBLISH(world_owner(), round_started)             // allocates nothing when nobody listens

observe(source, /datum/notice/hit/melee, src, then(PROC_REF(saw_hit)))   // handlers run on the listener, with A.target = source
unobserve(source, /datum/notice/hit/melee, src)
  • FIXED marks an action nothing can refuse, which is all a plain announcement is. It has no ACT_TRY; PUBLISH sends it.
  • world_owner() is the kernel's world entity. World-wide notices are published on it, and systems hook them with on_notice.
  • WANTS(E, /datum/notice/x) is TRUE when something on E listens for that notice (for the committed outcome; WANTS(E, /datum/notice/x, ACT_REPLACED) asks about another). Test it before building an expensive payload; PUBLISH itself allocates nothing when nobody listens.
  • observe() is an activation (X1): shorthand for granting source a hook_capability(on_notice(notice, parts...)) (section 11) with the listener as its source. Revoking it is unobserve(), and deleting either end ends it. In an observe, PROC_REF runs on the listener, so A.holder is the listener and A.target is the observed source. For a lasting effect on the source, grant it a capability directly: it is the same mechanism with one teardown.

An occurrence whose emitter reads a result back ("did anyone object?", "what's your armour?") is a question, not an event. It becomes a requirement on an action, a stat, or an accessor proc. Events with no listener are deleted with their emit sites.

Replaces:

  • OM events: OM_EMIT, OM_EMIT_WORLD, om_hook/om_unhook/om_hooked, /datum/om/event/* (355 types), before/* veto events, EVENT_HANDLER.
  • Guards: guard(), guarded(), GUARD_* keys, before_op on guard keys.
  • Op and damage hooks: before_op/after_op on ops (now requirements and on_notice), damage rows before_op(damage(...)), DAMAGE_REACTION* (a takeover is instead, a modification is adjusts), REFLECTS.
  • Bridges and overrides: on_channel, power_change() overrides.

11Capabilities

A capability is a named, parameterised bundle of entries: ops, contributions, triggers, look layers, examine lines, slots and UI data. It can have typed per-instance data and procs of its own. It's the unit of reuse: anything two types share is a capability, and anything only one type does is entries on that type.

Defining one

CAPABILITY_DEF(name, CAP_X, key =, stacks =, param = default, ...) // a body that returns entries; generates name(param = default, ...) CAPABILITY_TYPE(name, CAP_X, /datum/capability/x, key =, stacks =, param = default, ...) // a datum with code of its own; generates the same kind of constructor cap_keys(CAP_X, KEY = MSG(reason), ...) // the capability's state: ids, accessors, reasons, read registration
// The capability's state. Generates COVER_OPEN and COVER_REMOVED, cover_open(holder) and cover_removed(holder),
// a default reason for each, and the read registration. There is no hand-written READS_AS for capability state.
cap_keys(CAP_COVER, OPEN = MSG(cover/closed), REMOVED = MSG(cover/removed))

/// A cover over the holder's insides (the door of SPACE_HATCH). The params ARE the settings; key = name makes the name the selector,
/// so a type with two covers writes cover("panel") and cover("hatch").
CAPABILITY_DEF(cover, CAP_COVER, key = name, name = "cover", open = tool(TOOL_CROWBAR), remove = null, replace = null)
	return list(
		op("open", open, toggles(COVER_OPEN), when(not(COVER_REMOVED))),
		remove ? op("remove", remove, label("Remove cover"), needs(req_is(COVER_REMOVED, FALSE)),
			sets(COVER_REMOVED, TRUE), sets(COVER_OPEN, TRUE)) : null,
		replace ? op("replace", replace, label("Replace cover"),
			when(any_of(COVER_REMOVED, not(STAT_OPERABLE))), sets(COVER_REMOVED, FALSE)) : null,
		look_layer(LOOK_COVER_OPEN, when = COVER_OPEN),
		examine_line(MSG(cover/open_examine), when = COVER_OPEN),
		examine_line(MSG(cover/removed_examine), when = COVER_REMOVED))

// A capability with code of its own is a datum. Its procs run as CAP_PROC(x), x(datum/act/A), on the capability datum,
// with A.holder the entity and A.cap this capability. powered(channel) sits over powered_by(system, role): there is one definition.
CAPABILITY_TYPE(powered, CAP_POWERED, /datum/capability/powered, key = NONE, channel = POWER_CHANNEL_EQUIPMENT)   // the macro declares var/channel on the datum, with this default
/datum/capability/powered/entries()
	return list(
		powered_by(/datum/system/power, role = POWER_ROLE_LOAD),
		contributes(STAT_OPERABLE, CAP_PROC(has_power), reason = MSG(power/unpowered)))
/datum/capability/powered/proc/has_power(datum/act/A)
	var/obj/machinery/M = A.holder
	return M.power_area?.channel_on(channel)         // the hop through power_area is a generated read
  • Params are the settings. A definition's params are what configure() can change: configure(cover(remove = force_pry())) re-runs the body with that param replaced. An unknown param is a build error. There is no settings = list() to keep in step with the constructor. A param can name a var or a proc (PROC_REF), which is resolved per instance at runtime, and it can declare a schema (X5): recharge = num(1 SECOND, 5 MINUTES, default = 30 SECONDS), checked when configure() is built. A param's default has one home. With a schema it is the schema's default =, and without one it is the param = default of the definition line (channel = POWER_CHANNEL_EQUIPMENT). Either way CAPABILITY_TYPE declares var/<param> on the datum itself, as it does for a schema'd param, so the datum body never repeats a param's var line, and the schemas lint rejects one.
  • Two definition forms. CAPABILITY_DEF is entries only; its body is a generated global proc, so a holder proc it names is TYPE_PROC_REF(/type, x), as in any bundle. CAPABILITY_TYPE is for a capability that needs code: its vars are the params, entries() re-runs on configure, its procs are CAP_PROC(x), and the macro sets key = CAP_X on the datum. configure(CAP_X, variant = /datum/capability/x/other) swaps the datum type and keeps the params.
  • Op keys are namespaced. The ops a capability brings are prefixed with its name, the first argument of its definition: cover's "open" is "cover.open", and CAPABILITY_DEF(reagent_container, CAP_REAGENT_CONTAINER, ...) brings "reagent_container.inject". A capability that has a selector (below) adds it: "cell_bay.cell.insert"; a selector at its default value adds nothing, so cover() is "cover.open" and cover("hatch") is "cover.hatch.open". The prefix is the capability that declares the op, not the bundle that included it, so a hatch's cover still opens as "cover.open". extend("cover.open", ...) changes one op and extend(CAP_COVER, ...) all of them.
  • State keys are generated. cap_keys declares a capability's state, and each key is a boolean the capability's ops set (toggles(), sets()). Each key's accessor is <cap>_<key>(holder): cover_open, cover_removed, panel_open, lock_locked, emag_emagged. cap_keys() allocates one word per capability, and a word holds at most 24 keys; more is a build error naming the capability. A fact that several sources can force is a STAT, not a key. Each row's MSG is the default reason for the requirement the library itself writes on that key: req_is(COVER_OPEN) says the cover is closed, and req_is(COVER_REMOVED, FALSE) says it has been removed. A requirement written the other way round gives because =. READS_AS stays only for producer keys that aren't capability state, and the build checks that the accessor's own reads are covered by the key it names.
  • Lifecycle hooks: on_holder_preinit(A), on_holder_init(A) and on_holder_destroy(A), all x(datum/act/A) with A.holder and A.cap set, and A.mapload while the map loads. The order inside Initialize is: on_holder_preinit (before the base body runs, for work the parent's init reads: a part made in nullspace, an inherited icon cleared), the base body, on_holder_init, then the type's own code after its ..(). A granted capability has on_activate(A) when its activation attaches and on_deactivate(A) when it is removed, the cleanup hook of the one teardown path (below).
  • Per-activation data: cap_data(A) returns the typed /datum/cap_data/<cap> of A.activation, allocated on first use: for a type-level capability A.activation is null until it has state, and cap_data(A) creates the activation and the data together. Two activations of one capability on one holder have separate data. Declare the data type next to the capability (/datum/cap_data/cover, whose state keys live in it), and assign the result to a typed var: cap_data() is RETURN_TYPE(/datum/cap_data), and test builds check that var's type against the capability's declared data type. No more cap_data(holder, KEY)["x"]. A field a condition reads is a declared tracked field of that data type (section 7). Code outside a trigger reaches a capability with cap_of(E, CAP_X), an indexed lookup that returns the datum of the type-level activation, or null. The accessors <cap>_<key>(holder) address the type-level activation too, so neither can say which of several grants is meant. A capability that is granted at runtime (named by grant(), while_slotted, species_capabilities or observe) and has state (cap_keys or a cap_data type) therefore declares a selector other than NONE, which the accessors and cap_of(E, CAP_X, selector) take; the table builder checks the literal grant sites. Its own handlers always reach their own state through A.activation.
  • Changing one: configure(x(param = v)) rebuilds its entries from the new params; extend("cover.open", ...) changes one op and extend(CAP_X, ...) all of them (drop = "id" relaxes a requirement the capability named, replaces(parts...) swaps an effect list); without(CAP_X) drops it. Any entry inside a capability can carry key =, so these can address it.
  • Granting one: grant(E, what, source, lasts) creates an activation of a capability (a type, or a constructor call such as cover()) on one instance at runtime, and returns it, or null with the reason logged when the attach fails (section 5). Activations are a second table beside the type's own, interned by (type table, sorted definitions), so a grant or revoke swaps a table id. Type-level extend(CAP_X, ...) and without(CAP_X) address a capability by key, so they apply to a granted one the same way.
  • A hook capability is hook_capability(entries...): a capability of triggers only, with no key of its own. observe() activates one on the observed entity with the listener as its source (section 10).

Keys and repeats

A capability's key is (CAP_X, selector). The definition declares its selector explicitly with key =: the name of the param that tells two uses of the capability on one type apart, or NONE when there is only ever one. Nothing is inferred from parameter order, so changing a constructor's signature never changes its identity. An identical repeat (the same interned signature) dedupes. The same key with different params is a build error naming both lines, which the table builder knows because it records __FILE__:__LINE__ for every entry. (A second CAPABILITIES list for a type is a build error too.)

A selector is written as the first argument: cover("panel") and cover("hatch") are two covers on one type. configure(cover("hatch", remove = force_pry())), without(CAP_COVER, "panel"), the state accessors cover_open(holder, "hatch") and the key id of a non-default selector, all take it. A bare COVER_OPEN is always the id of the default selector's key. A non-default selector's id is the generated COVER_HATCH_OPEN, or in function form cover_key(COVER_OPEN, "hatch"): no macro is ever written both with and without arguments. With one instance of the capability the selector may be left out.

Capabilitykey =A second one is
space(SPACE_X, inside =, door =, applies_to =, ...)the space idanother space: both stand
cell_bay(var, at =)the varanother bay on another var: both stand
toggle_setting(nameof(v), name, parts...)the vara toggle for another var: both stand
powered_by(system, role)the systemmembership in another system: both stand
interface(window, ...)the windowanother window: both stand; its open op is "interface.<window>.open"
cover(name = "cover", ...)nameanother cover under another name: both stand (cover("hatch"))
panel(), lock(...), emag(...), construction(...), deployment(...)NONE (each has its own CAP_X, so one type may carry a build graph and a deployment graph)an identical repeat dedupes; different params are a build error

Definition, activation, contributions

A capability has three parts that earlier drafts ran together. Naming them answers what two items granting the same capability mean.

PartWhat it isWhat belongs to it
DefinitionImmutable and interned: the CAPABILITY_DEF or CAPABILITY_TYPE with its paramsThe entries it builds, its schemas, its stacking policy
ActivationOne per (definition key, source, holder): foundation X1 (section 5). A type-level capability has one per holder, created lazilyIts params, its per-activation state (cap_data), its scope, and the list of contributions it made
ContributionsWhat an activation put into its holder: stat contributions, hooks, ops, observes, outputs, owned grantsStat contributions combine through the stat's rule; behaviour runs under the stacking policy
 
CAPABILITY_TYPE(mirror_plating, CAP_MIRROR_PLATING, /datum/capability/mirror_plating,
	key = NONE, stacks = BEST(reflect_chance), reflect_chance = 30, absorb = 0.67)
stacks =Behaviour (hooks, rolls, ops, periodic work) when several activations are present
STACK (default)Runs once per activation
UNIQUERuns only the first activation, by attach order
BEST(param)Runs only the activation with the highest value of param. A tie goes to the first by attach order
  • Stat contributions never stack by this policy. They always combine through the stat's own rule, one contribution per activation, so two sources of density or light_range behave as the stat's rule says.
  • The two-mirrors answer. A wearer in a laserproof vest (30%) who holds a mirror shield (45%) has two activations of CAP_MIRROR_PLATING. Reflection is BEST(reflect_chance): one roll, at 45%, and A.source is the shield, the winning activation's source. The vest's activation is shadowed, not removed: take the shield off and it runs. The plating's adjusts comes from the winner too. For an op, STACK offers one op per activation (a menu names each source), and UNIQUE and BEST one.
  • A.source is the running activation's source. Ties go to the first activation by attach order.
  • Removal. Removing an activation removes all its contributions, runs its on_deactivate, and ends everything it owns: a hold or grant its ops placed on its holder, and child activations. They end with it unless declared outlives = TRUE. So when a species change drops the phase shift capability, the phased grant its op placed ends in the same step (16.9).
  • Attach is atomic in what it applies. The contributions are built and validated (schemas, the cycle check of section 7) before any stat is touched, so a failure leaves nothing applied, and grant() returns null with the reason logged. On success the stat contributions apply at once, and the hooks, ops, observes and outputs are registered at once and go live at the next drain. A revoked activation is dead at once, and one may end itself from its own handler, which finishes first (section 5).

Modes: exclusive state capabilities

CAPABILITIES(/obj/machinery/press)
	modes(nameof(mode))                                   // `mode` is a TRACKED var holding a capability type
	on_notice(/datum/notice/mode_changed, then(PROC_REF(heard_mode)))

CAPABILITY_TYPE(press_working, CAP_PRESS_WORKING, /datum/capability/press_working, key = NONE)
/datum/capability/press_working/entries()
	return list(
		every(2 SECONDS, then(CAP_PROC(stamp))),
		on_notice(/datum/notice/jammed, then(CAP_PROC(note_jam)), go(/datum/capability/press_jammed)),
		after_in_state(10 MINUTES, go(/datum/capability/press_idle)))     // a timer the state owns
  • One var, one active state. The var holds the type of the active capability. The engine grants it with the holder as the source, revokes the previous one, and publishes /datum/notice/mode_changed (old_mode, new_mode, mode_var) on the holder. The initial value of the var is granted when the holder initializes. A state is an ordinary capability: its every(), on_notice(), on_change(), coalesce() and contributes() entries run exactly while it is the mode and stop when it ends, by the one teardown path (above).
  • go(/datum/capability/x, var =) is a part that sets the mode through the setter of the var and follows it at once. The state that ran it ends immediately: the handler finishes, nothing of the state runs again, and the parts after go() in that entry do not run. Inside a state var defaults to the var that state is the mode of; a holder with several modes() names it.
  • after_in_state(delay, parts...) belongs to a state, never to the own list of a type (build error). It is armed on entry and cancelled on exit however the exit came.
  • Writing the var through its setter also works; the mode follows at the next drain. A value that is not a declared capability type is reported and put back; go(), modes() and after_in_state() refuse bad arguments when the declaration is built.
  • Transitions are sequential. A state whose activation goes straight on to another runs as a chain of transitions after the first has finished (at most MODES_MAX_CHAIN), never nested. mode_now(E, var) reads the current mode; mode_enter(E, var, type) sets one from code. GLOB.forms_trace logs every transition.
  • Uses. Machine idle, working and broken; the phases of a door; reactor modes; the calm, alert and engaged states of a mob (doc/rewrite/ai_packs.md).

Wires: composed from capabilities

A holder's wires are not a list it keeps. Each wire is defined once, in the WIRE_DEF table of the wires library (code/library/machine/wires.dm), with what cutting, pulsing and mending it does, written as effects on the capability that brings it. A capability brings its wires (brings_wires(): the capability type's TYPE_TABLE(..., brought_wires, ...), or its params), and wires() derives the holder's wire list from the capabilities it has. wires() keeps only what belongs to the wiring as a whole: the name (window title, blueprints, and the key of the round's shared layout), the count (the rest are duds), randomize, the space they sit in (at =), reach =, emp =, tools =, by_hand =, status_lines =, starts_cut =, and the window and record (window =, record =). count and randomize may name a holder proc (an airlock built with secure electronics).

A wire's effects run on each capability that brought it, then the wire's notice is published, so on_notice hooks see the state the wire left. The hold effects are keyed, sourced holds: each wire has one cut source and one pulse source (flyweights of its definition). A cut holds the capability's stat at cut_value, above any pulse; a pulse holds it at pulse_value for pulse_lasts (a second pulse refreshes the one hold; WIRE_PULSE_TOGGLES flips it instead; 0, the pulse does nothing); a pulse running out never undoes a cut; mending releases both. A capability given no stat brings its wire with no state of its own.

A wire only one type has is that type's own capability: on_wire(WIRE_X, cut = PROC_REF(a), pulse = PROC_REF(b)) brings WIRE_X and hooks it (a hears the cut and the mend, N.mended). On a wire a library capability also brings, an on_wire() adds to what the wire does there (the airlock's ID wire also flashes the deny light); it is for real deviations only.

WIRE_DEF(WIRE_AI_CONTROL, "AI Control", cut = wire_cut_holds(), pulse = wire_pulse_holds(), mend = wire_mend_releases())
WIRE_DEF(WIRE_DOOR_BOLTS, "Door Bolts", cut = wire_calls(TYPE_PROC_REF(/datum/capability/lib/bolts, bolt_wire_cut)),
	pulse = wire_calls(TYPE_PROC_REF(/datum/capability/lib/bolts, bolt_wire_pulsed)))

CAPABILITY_TYPE(ai_control, CAP_AI_CONTROL, /datum/capability/lib/ai_control, key = NONE, stat = null, pulse_lasts = 1 SECOND)
TYPE_TABLE(/datum/capability/lib/ai_control, brought_wires, list(WIRE_AI_CONTROL))

STAT(/obj/machinery/alarm, aidisabled, ANY)
CAPABILITIES(/obj/machinery/alarm)
	wires(name = "Air alarm", count = 5, tools = FALSE, status_lines = PROC_REF(wire_lights))
	power_wires(stat = STAT_SHORTED, pulse_lasts = 20 MINUTES, shock = 50)   // brings WIRE_MAIN_POWER1
	ai_control(stat = STAT_AIDISABLED, pulse_lasts = 10 SECONDS)            // brings WIRE_AI_CONTROL
	on_wire(WIRE_SYPHON, cut = PROC_REF(syphon_wire_cut), pulse = PROC_REF(syphon_wire_pulsed))   // the alarm's own

Bundles and capabilities

A bundle is any proc that returns entries or parts. Wrap it in a capability only when someone must address it: configure, extend, without or grant it as a unit. These three wire other capabilities together, and are capabilities, so without(CAP_MACHINE_BASICS) and configure(machine_basics(frame = NONE)) work:

CapabilityContents
machine_basics(board, anchored_by =, repair =, frame =, powered =)breakable, powered, anchor, the construction ladder frame (default machine_frame(board), NONE for no ladder), and extend(TAG_CONTROL, needs(req_operable())). The panel comes from whatever owns maintenance access (maintenance_hatch, service_panel(wires) or panel()), so declaring both here and in a hatch can't clash; machine_frame needs a panel and says so at boot if there isn't one
wall_machine(...)machine_basics without anchoring, plus wall_mount
maintenance_hatch(cover =, wires =, emag =, lock = TRUE, lock_wire =, panel_needs_cover_closed = FALSE)space(SPACE_HATCH, door = CAP_COVER), the cover you pass (default cover()), panel (with its SPACE_PANEL), the wires(...) entry when given, lock(wire = lock_wire) (the lock brings that wire and needs it intact), emag(emag) when given, and the rules between them (the ID lock and the emag work only with the cover and panel closed, req_closed(); the panel is latched shut while the cover is open when panel_needs_cover_closed). A machine passes its wires entry and its emag effect here instead of declaring wires and emag again

Plain bundles stay plain: a bundle is a global proc (console(board), atmos_device(uses_power, unwrench_delay), force_pry(), component_swap(type, delay)), for a small snippet several types share. It returns entries or parts, nests freely, and can't be addressed as a unit; the capabilities inside it can. Being global, it names holder procs TYPE_PROC_REF(/type, x). One whose first statement is return list(entries) is read by the analysis like a block, so generated reads, UI types, op keys and op order see into a name() entry. There is no BUNDLE marker: what one type alone groups is a section of its own block (section 1), so a large machine reads

CAPABILITIES(/obj/machinery/power/apc)
	wall_machine(/obj/item/module/power_control, repair = NONE, frame = apc_frame(), powered = FALSE)
	...
	on_change(nameof(cell), ANY, then(PROC_REF(cell_changed)))

	section(controls, "The APC's window and the buttons in it")
	interface("APC")
	op("breaker", ui_act(), toggles(nameof(operating)), then(PROC_REF(settings_applied)), logs(LOG_GAME))
	...
	section(cover_rules, "What the hatch asks of the APC beyond the library's rules")
	extend("cell_bay.cell.take", when(COVER_OPEN))
	...

The table builder flattens bundles in list order and ignores section headings, so neither changes what is compiled; a section only changes what Explain prints.

Replaces:

  • Every library constructor's private gating parameters (needs, else_say, works_*, log, open_tool, ...); cap_gating().
  • capability(KEY, entries..., settings = list()) and its hand-copied settings list; lib_op and the OP_SHAPE_* presets; /datum/capability/condition (now a capability granted with a duration).
  • Hand-written capability state: bit words such as COVER_OPEN | COVER_REMOVED, req_set/req_clear on them, and their READS_AS accessors (cover_is_open, panel_is_open, is_locked, is_emagged).
  • The untyped cap_data(holder, KEY)["x"], per-holder capability data (now per activation), and a discriminator inferred from the first required param (now key =).
  • The ~40 holder-interface procs on /atom (B2); RefreshParts() and parts(slots) (now components(slots)); deconstruct(board) and the machine dismantle = parameter (now frame =).
  • emag_act() and DECLARE_EMAG*; EMP_DISABLE, REFLECTS and hand-rolled EMP code; block(chance) (now blocks(chance)); the reach(range) item capability (now reach = on a provider); label() (now labelable(), since label("text") names an op).
  • om_attach behaviours; cap_trait(...) (now trait(...)).

Machines

A machine's periodic work is an every() gated by what it needs, so one that cannot work parks and costs nothing: started_work(step =, when =, gate =, wakes_on =) for work something starts and the work ends, every(interval, then(...), when = STAT_OPERABLE) for work that runs whenever the machine works. There is no machine pipeline, machine_step() or MACHINE_WAKE(): a change of the state a gate reads (wakes_on, a stat, a tracked var) un-parks the work, and work_start() / work_stop() start and end started work by hand. A machine works when STAT_OPERABLE holds: the power grid's reading (powered), breakage, EMP holds and a maintenance panel each contribute to it. The grid (powernets, pipenets, SMES, APCs) stays on its own model behind power_change() / set_powered(), which is the one adapter that writes the powered state; a machine's draw is the use_power mode and its idle and active usage, which the machine contributes to its area's demand stats (contributes_to(nameof(power_area), STAT_DEMAND_EQUIP and its light and environment twins, SUM): the area's demand is the sum of its machines' contributions and nothing is tallied (doc/rewrite/power_grid.md). The stat bits themselves (NOPOWER, BROKEN, MAINT, EMPED) are still the stored form, reaching STAT_OPERABLE through the one stat_bits_allow() bridge until each becomes its owning capability's contribution.

12Construction

STATE_GRAPH(GRAPH_X) start(STAGE_X) stage(STAGE_X, parts..., from =, undo =, key =) // one per stage dismantle(...) at(SPACE_X) // a named, reusable graph construction(GRAPH_X | start(STAGE_X), stage(...), ..., dismantle(parts..., ruined(condition, parts...)), at(SPACE_X)) // a build ladder: a graph with a ledger and an operability contribution deployment(GRAPH_X | start(STAGE_X), stage(...), ...) // a deployable (a barricade, a folding bed, a turret): the same graph under its own key CAP_DEPLOYMENT, its ledger refunding like a build's STAGE_DEF(group, name) // defines STAGE_<GROUP>_<NAME> and the stage key "<group>_<name>"; its text is MSG_DEF(stage/<group>/<name>, ...) built(E, STAGE_X) // TRUE when STAGE_X is the current stage or is on the path from the start to it, through E's history built_material(E, STAGE_X) // the material actually used at that stage, from the ledger; null if it took none // ruined(): when the condition holds, dismantling yields its parts instead (scrap rather than a reusable frame)

Construction and deployment are state graphs, foundation X4 (below): stages are nodes, ops are edges, and each instance keeps a current stage, a history of the transitions it took and a ledger of what each took in. A build stage is an op (construction.build:<stage>) whose effect is "advance to this stage". Its undo is another op (construction.undo:<stage>), derived from the input part. Stage ids come from STAGE_DEF, so a misspelt stage is a compile error. The stage id is the first argument, and from =, undo = and key = are named, so they come last. "Stage" here always means a build stage; the four steps of an op are op stages (section 9).

STAGE_DEF(intercom, frame)
STAGE_DEF(intercom, wired)
STAGE_DEF(intercom, boarded)
STAGE_DEF(intercom, secured)

/proc/intercom_frame()
	return construction(start(STAGE_INTERCOM_FRAME),
		stage(STAGE_INTERCOM_WIRED, stack(/obj/item/stack/cable_coil, 5)),
		stage(STAGE_INTERCOM_BOARDED, item(/obj/item/circuitboard/intercom), put_in(SLOT_CONSTRUCTION),
			then(TYPE_PROC_REF(/obj/item/radio/intercom, seat_board))),     // a global bundle names holder procs by type
		stage(STAGE_INTERCOM_SECURED, tool(TOOL_SCREWDRIVER)),
		dismantle(tool(TOOL_WELDER), becomes(/obj/item/frame/intercom)))

CAPABILITIES(/obj/item/radio/intercom)
	intercom_frame()
	extend("construction.build:intercom_wired", wait(4 SECONDS))         // the stage key is the STAGE_ id without its prefix, lowercase
ForwardDerived undoRefund (the ledger entry of the transition being undone)
item(T) with put_in(SLOT_CONSTRUCTION)An empty hand takes it out (take_out(SLOT_CONSTRUCTION))The same item: SLOT_CONSTRUCTION is the graph's own slot, so inserted items are its owned contents
stack(T, n)The material's tool (cable: wirecutters; sheets: wrench or welder)n units of the material actually used, as the ledger recorded it at build
tool(Q)The same tool, reversed verbNothing
item(T) with consumes()The material's tool, if the item names one; else undo = (the door kit below names none)The consumed item, rebuilt from its ledger entry (its type and material, recorded when the cost was reserved)
Anything elseundo = list(parts...), or undo = null for no way backFrom its parts

Write undo = only when the way back differs from the derived one. undo = list(tool(TOOL_WRENCH)) after tool(TOOL_WRENCH) adds nothing.

  • Hooks know their direction: then() going forward, undone() going back. undone() is a graph-only part, like ruined(). A preset is a global proc, so the holder procs it names are TYPE_PROC_REF(/type, x), never PROC_REF(x).
  • Dismantling: dismantle(parts...) runs its parts, then the holder goes: becomes(T) replaces it, and without becomes() it is deleted (any spawns() are dropped first). It refunds the whole ledger.
  • Branches: from = STAGE_X or from = list(STAGE_X, STAGE_Y) lets one edge leave from several stages (girder to wall, or girder to reinforced wall). A stage reached by different inputs gets one stage() entry per way in, the second and later with key =. A stage that works at any point is from = ANY_STAGE. A from that names a stage the graph doesn't declare is a boot error.
  • Keys: the build op of an edge is "construction.build:<stage>" (".<key>" appended for a second way in), the undo op is one per stage, "construction.undo:<stage>", and the teardown "construction.dismantle". A deployment graph uses its own prefix, "deployment.deploy:<stage>", "deployment.undeploy:<stage>" and "deployment.dismantle". extend, without and perform_op address them, and the build resolves each literal against the declared stages.
  • Priority: when the same tool both builds and undoes from a stage, build wins a click and the menu offers both. A graph that wants the undo to win a click says so on that stage with extend("construction.undo:<stage>", priority(above("construction.build:<other>"))).
  • State: per instance, in the activation's data, the graph keeps the current stage (a STAGE_* id), the history stack and the ledger. built(E, STAGE_X) and req_built(STAGE_X) read them, so a typo can't compile. start(STAGE_X) is the graph's starting stage, and a subtype that begins part-built writes configure(construction_graph(start = STAGE_X)) for the stage it is placed at. The instance then stores only its current stage: the history is seeded lazily, the first time something reads or pops it, along the path that leads from the graph's start to the placed stage. If exactly one path leads there, that path is seeded. If several do (a stage with two ways in, like the finished door below), the type must name the one it stands for, configure(construction_graph(start = STAGE_X, via = list(STAGE_A, STAGE_B))), the stages passed in order between the graph's start and the placed stage, and a placed stage with several paths and no via = is a build error. Seeded entries carry empty ledger entries, so undoing past the seed refunds nothing, and a map-placed finished machine allocates no history until then.
  • Operability: a construction contributes req_built_final() to STAT_OPERABLE, so an unfinished machine doesn't work, and no type keeps its own maintenance flag.
  • Text: a stage's description is the MSG_DEF(stage/<group>/<name>, ...) that STAGE_DEF requires, and messages come from the tool profile's verbs. "Next: wrench it into place" is the next stage's message.
  • Space: at(SPACE_X) says which space of the holder the graph works inside, so every step and its undo needs the path to it open: set aside while another candidate answers, refused with the door's reason when none does (section 8). A protrudes(because = ...) among a stage's parts keeps that space's door from closing while the graph is at the stage. A space declared from_stage = STAGE_X exists only from that stage on.
  • Conservation: the round-trip test covers every graph, branches included: building to the end and undoing back to the start returns the instance to the stage, the history and the ledger it began with, and returns what it took.
  • Presets are plain bundles that return a construction(...), or a STATE_GRAPH when they take no params: machine_frame(board), girder(), mech_chassis(...), door_assembly(). A machine takes one through machine_basics(frame = ...) or declares it directly.

State graphs (X4)

One graph form carries construction, deconstruction and deployment. A graph is nodes (STAGE_DEF stages) and edges (the ops that move between them); an instance of it keeps three things, and each has one job.

Per instanceHoldsAnswers
Current stageOne STAGE_* idWhere the instance is now; what edges leave it
History stackThe transitions actually taken, in order, each naming its edgeWhich predecessor to undo to; whether a stage is "at or past" (below)
LedgerWhat each transition took: the material used, the item inserted, the resources spent (X2, section 9). An entry is written when the edge's costs are reserved and finalised at commit, so the edge's own effects can read itWhat to refund when a transition is undone, and built_material(), which inside an edge's own then() already sees that edge's entry
  • Undo pops the history. It returns to the stage the instance actually came from, the top of the history, and refunds exactly that transition's ledger entry. A declared from only says which edges exist, so a stage with several ways in undoes to the right one. There is one undo op per stage whichever way in the instance took: its inputs are the union of the inputs of the edges into the stage, and the edge on top of the history decides which refund and which undone() hooks apply. An edge with no derivable undo and no undo = contributes no input to the union, so a stage reached only by such edges has no undo op, as with undo = null.
  • "At or past stage X" is reachability on the graph from the start through the history: built(E, X) is TRUE when X is the current stage or appears in the history. A stage the instance never passed, because it took another branch, is not built.
  • The ledger and the history are separate. The ledger records what was put in, for refunds; the history records where the instance has been. Neither stands in for the other.
  • Machine modes are not graphs. A pump's off, on and syphon, or a vending machine's idle and vending, are plain tracked enums with a schema (section 4). A graph is for a progression with undo and a ledger.
  • Deployment is the same graph. A folding bed, a barricade or a turret on its tripod is a deployment(...): stages are folded and deployed, the edges are ops with their requirements and costs, and folding back pops the history. It has its own capability key, CAP_DEPLOYMENT, and its own op prefix (deployment.deploy:<stage>, deployment.undeploy:<stage>), so a type may carry a build graph and a deployment graph without a clash. It has no operability contribution of its own, and what it spends it records in the ledger like a build: undeploying refunds it.
STAGE_DEF(door, frame)
STAGE_DEF(door, wired)
STAGE_DEF(door, boarded)
STAGE_DEF(door, finished)

STATE_GRAPH(GRAPH_DOOR_ASSEMBLY)
	start(STAGE_DOOR_FRAME)
	stage(STAGE_DOOR_WIRED, stack(/obj/item/stack/cable_coil, 5))
	stage(STAGE_DOOR_BOARDED, item(/obj/item/circuitboard/airlock), put_in(SLOT_CONSTRUCTION))
	stage(STAGE_DOOR_FINISHED, tool(TOOL_SCREWDRIVER), from = STAGE_DOOR_BOARDED)   // "construction.build:door_finished"
	stage(STAGE_DOOR_FINISHED, item(/obj/item/door_kit), consumes(), from = STAGE_DOOR_WIRED, key = "kit",    // a prefab kit skips the board: "...door_finished.kit"
		undo = list(tool(TOOL_CROWBAR)))   // a consumed kit names no tool, so the way back is written: pry the kit out
	dismantle(tool(TOOL_WELDER), becomes(/obj/item/frame/door))

// History (oldest first)                    built(E, STAGE_DOOR_BOARDED)   "construction.undo:door_finished" (screwdriver or crowbar) goes to
// frame > wired > boarded > finished         TRUE                           boarded: the board stays in its slot, nothing is refunded
// frame > wired > finished (kit)             FALSE                          wired: the ledger entry of the kit edge refunds one door kit
//
// A door assembly placed finished by a map has two paths to that stage, so its type names the one it stands for:
//   CAPABILITIES(/obj/structure/door_assembly)
//       construction(GRAPH_DOOR_ASSEMBLY)
//   CAPABILITIES(/obj/structure/door_assembly/finished)
//       configure(construction_graph(start = STAGE_DOOR_FINISHED, via = list(STAGE_DOOR_WIRED, STAGE_DOOR_BOARDED)))      // seeded lazily as frame > wired > boarded > finished: built(E, STAGE_DOOR_BOARDED) is TRUE

Replaces: cap_construction with stage() (24 parameters), branch() (16) and ladder_options() (21); the construction step() and string stage names; insert(T) as a construction input (now item(T) with put_in()); build_insert/build_wire/build_fasten/build_weld/build_step/build_any_order; ladder_dismantle; /datum/construction_graph (16 files); state-var ladders in tool-act overrides; stage-name comparisons in hooks; hand-written refund hooks and "at or past" comparisons of stage order; a separate deployable state machine per type.

13Look, UI, verbs, prompts

Look

/obj/machinery/power/apc/draw(datum/look/look)      // an output: reads generated, runs once per frame after a read changed
	look.variant("broken", !operable && integrity_broken)
	look.layer("charging", charging)
	look.glow("screen", operable ? "on" : null)

CAPABILITIES(/obj/machinery/light)   // excerpt: the full list is in 16.11
	look_layer("bulb-broken", when = nameof(bulb_broken))   // declarative layers need no draw() at all
	sound_loop(SFX_LIGHT_HUM, when = nameof(on))

// A disguise rewrites what the thing is called; a body is one composite of named layers, cached by what changes it.
/obj/item/clothing/mask/chameleon/draw(datum/look/look)
	look.identity(name = disguise_name, desc = disguise_desc)
/mob/living/carbon/human/draw(datum/look/look)
	look.composite("body", key = list(species.id, skin_tone, body_markings), layers = species.body_layers)
  • Look builder: the only writer of overlays, underlays, icon state, emissives, levels, names and descriptions. Its calls are look.variant(name, on), look.layer(name, on, under = FALSE), look.glow(name, state), look.level(plane, layer), look.identity(name =, desc =) and look.composite(name, key =, layers =), plus the transforms look.set_dir(), set_color(), set_alpha() and set_transform(). Everything else on /datum/look is internal. A builder call takes a value, and an entry takes a condition, so when = appears only on entries (look_layer, sound_loop, examine_line).
  • Output order: the engine runs a type's capability outputs first, then its own draw, ui_data, push_to_rust and examine, so an override does not call ..() to get the capabilities. Where a parent's body remains (a base type's draw), that proc is SHOULD_CALL_PARENT(TRUE), and DreamChecker flags a subtype that forgets. The standard outputs have fixed arguments: draw(datum/look/look), ui_data(datum/act/A), examine(datum/act/A) and push_to_rust(); any other output declared with outputs() takes (datum/act/A).
  • Names, descriptions and underlays: a computed name or description is look.identity(), and an underlay is look.layer(name, on, under = TRUE). Light is not a look call: STAT_LIGHT_RANGE (and the light's colour and power, declared the same way) are stats the engine applies, so look.light() is gone.
  • Composites: a layered body (species body, limbs, markings, hair, each slot's clothing) is built by look.composite() from named layers and cached by key, the list of inputs that change it, in the shared composite cache. Only the composite whose key changed is rebuilt. A worn item adds its layer to the wearer's composite through while_slotted(SLOT_ANY_WORN, look_layer(...), on = ON_HOLDER). overlays_standing and the hand-rolled mutable /icon caches go away.
  • Icon state names follow the standard dashed convention (<base>-<variant>, <base>-<layer>), checked by look_lacks() and the icon tests.
  • Flashes: a short-lived overlay is a flash(layer) feedback part, or look_flash() from code.
  • Banned: update_icon(), add_overlay and queue_icon_update() in content.

Examine

examine_line(text | PROC_REF, when =) entries, plus examine(datum/act/A) as an on-demand output for anything computed (A.actor is the examiner). Capabilities contribute their own lines (cover state, construction progress, "Next: wrench it into place"), and the engine adds them before the type's examine() runs.

UI

CAPABILITIES(/obj/machinery/power/apc)   // excerpt: the full list is in 16.1
	interface("APC", title = "Area Power Controller")   // declares the window and adds its open op
	ui_shape(operating, channels = list_of(row(name = text(24), mode = enum(POWERCHAN_MODES))))   // a var named here takes its schema (section 4)
	op("set_channel", ui_act(arg("channel", enum(APC_CHANNELS)), arg("mode", enum(POWERCHAN_MODES))),
		then(PROC_REF(set_channel_mode)))
	op("breaker", ui_act(), toggles(nameof(operating)))   // ui_act() takes the op's key as its action name
// No lock clause: lock() gates every TAG_UI op (section 11).

/obj/machinery/power/apc/proc/set_channel_mode(datum/act/A, channel, mode)   // UI args arrive validated, as typed parameters; the build checks the names against arg()
	...

/obj/machinery/power/apc/ui_data(datum/act/A)       // an output: pushed at most once per tick, only to open windows, only when a read changed
	return list("operating" = operating, "channels" = channel_list())

// Generated from the schemas of ui_shape and of the declared ops (build.sh ui-types), so the interface can't drift from the server:
//   type ApcData = { operating: boolean; channels: { name: string; mode: 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 }[] };   // the four POWERCHAN_* values
//   type ApcActions = { breaker: {}; set_channel: { channel: string; mode: 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 } };
  • UI buttons are ops. Their arguments are validated against their schemas (X5) before the handler runs, by the same validator the prompts use. The same requirements apply as for clicking: a locked APC refuses the button and the click alike, and the reason shows in the window.
  • Opening: interface(window, title =, rights =, host =, input = hand()) declares the window and adds its open op, "interface.<window>.open", bound to input and to remote(), so a silicon opens the window remotely; extend("interface.<window>.open", inputs(hand())) narrows it to hands. rights = adds req_rights(), and host = PROC_REF redirects to another datum. opens_ui() is the effect part for an op with a different input, and ui_open(E, actor) from code is perform_op(actor, E, "interface.<window>.open"). Code that ran as a window opened (the old ui_opening and ui_opened) is on_op("interface.<window>.open", parts...).
  • Window access is a requirement. Range, consciousness and rights are requirements on the open op, with generated reads, and the window stays open only while they hold. Walking away or fainting refuses one, and the window closes showing the reason. Nothing polls.
  • Topic links are ops. A link in a browser window or a chat line (an href) names its op with topic("key", args...), and the input inbox runs it as it runs a click: client/Topic() submits a typed /datum/input_event/topic, which calls op_topic_href(actor, holder, href_list), and the op runs with origin ORIGIN_UI through the same Match, Require, Wait and Do (the actor is A.actor, never usr; the raw href is A.topic_href()). The topic key is the href key, so no link in a page changes: topic("take") answers ?src=[REF(x)];take=1, and topic("action=Reply") answers only action=Reply (a key=value key is tried before a bare key, as a TOPIC_ACTION row was). Every value of the href is an arg, read by name and checked by its schema before the handler runs: arg("n", int(0, 9)), arg("note", schema_text(64)), arg("mode", num()). A value the link may leave out is optional = TRUE (the handler gets null); a ref arg is the text of a ref, arg("who", schema_ref(/mob/living), among = TOPIC_IN_MOBS), and is looked up only among its source (TOPIC_IN_MOBS, TOPIC_IN_WORLD, TOPIC_IN_CONTENTS, TOPIC_IN_CLIENTS or a PROC_REF on the holder that returns the list), never anywhere locate() reaches. A value the schema cannot read refuses the link (the actor is told "That isn't something you can enter."); a number past its range is clamped and logged. Rights are needs(req_rights(R_X)), and a rights refusal on a link is also logged and sent to the admins, as an exploit attempt. Who may use the holder's links at all is a requirement (needs(req(PROC_REF(x), silent = TRUE)), req_self() for a window only its own mob uses); the holder's topic_allowed(user, href_list) gate still runs first, for the types that have not moved it into needs(). A holder whose topic_forward() names another datum hands it every href none of its own ops names (a codex page's link goes to its book). A link that waits for an answer is an asks() step of its op; until its handler is converted, topic_ask() re-runs the href through the same dispatch with the answer in it. topic_in(NAMESPACE, "key", args...) is a link in a separate href namespace that no plain Topic() reaches (the View Variables actions, VV_TOPIC on the dropdown's datum and VV_ADMIN_TOPIC on the admin's holder); the dispatch that names the namespace brings its own gate. A test drives a link with inbox_topic(actor, holder, href_list), which returns the op's /datum/op_result.
  • Data and shape: ui_data(datum/act/A) returns the data, and ui_shape(...) declares its fields with schemas (X5, section 4): a field that names a tracked var takes that var's schema (ui_shape(operating)), and a computed field gives one (channels = list_of(row(name = text(24), mode = enum(POWERCHAN_MODES)))). TypeScript types are generated from those schemas and from the window's ops, including each op's name and typed args: int and num become number with their range in a doc comment (/** int 0..100 */), enum a union of literals, text a string with its length in a doc comment. Test builds validate every push against the shape. A capability's own data is merged under caps.<key> and has its own ui_shape. ui_static_data(datum/act/A) is cached.
  • Transport: the tgui transport is a host system (section 2) that owns the window pool, prewarm, assets and open, close and transfer. A window is pushed in phase R after a read it uses changed, at most once per window per tick. The polling fire() and the update_uis INVOKE_ASYNC are deleted.
  • Banned: SStgui.update_uis, tgui_act/tgui_interact overrides, and hand parsing of params.

Verbs and abilities

// A gameplay ability is an op. menu() lists it under the one "Abilities" verb-panel entry;
// button and bind add an action button and a keybind.
CAPABILITY_DEF(regeneration, CAP_REGENERATION, key = NONE, recharge = num(1 SECOND, 5 MINUTES, default = 30 SECONDS))
	return list(
		op("regenerate", menu(button = "regenerate", bind = "shift+r"),
			cooldown(recharge), then(TYPE_PROC_REF(/mob/living, regenerate))))   // a definition body names holder procs by type
  • Abilities are ops, never verbs. Every menu() op the actor can use is listed under one verb-panel entry, "Abilities", with refused ones greyed and their reasons. button = gives the op an action button on the HUD whose state (ready, cooling down, refused) is an output with generated reads; a cooldown(t) that the button displays schedules an invalidation at its expiry, so the light comes on by itself (section 7). An ability is the actor's own op: it is on the actor's channel, so no reach test applies (section 8). bind = registers a keybind that performs the op, through the input inbox like any key. A bind that collides with another is a boot error, unless the two ops' when parts are mutually exclusive (the exemption of the same-input rule, section 8): shift and unshift in 16.9 share one key that way. A species, trait or item gives an ability by granting the capability that holds the op.
  • Verbs are for client, OOC and admin commands only: verb_entry(path, login =, when =) on a type. The layering lint rejects verb_entry under code/content.
  • Runtime verbs: grant(E, granted_verb(path), source), and grant(E, hidden_verb(path), source) to hide one.
  • The verb store is the only writer of a verbs list.
  • Admin and debug commands: ADMIN_VERB(name, rights, "Name", "Description", VERB_CAT_X, args...) and DEBUG_VERB(...) with the same arguments, plus admin_require() / admin_can(). The body receives the calling client as user, so it never reads usr (16.9). Categories are the VERB_CAT_* defines: the admin, debug, mapping, server, OOC and preferences families stay for verb entries, and the 50 VERB_CAT_ABILITIES_* categories go with the ability verbs, now ops under "Abilities". The VERB_CAT_IC_* categories are sorted by the A8 classifier (section 19): an IC verb that is gameplay becomes an op under "Abilities" (menu()), and only a real client command keeps verb_entry() and an IC category.

Requests, prompts and workflows (X3)

// Inside any trigger. asks() is a step of the op's Wait workflow: every answer is in before costs are reserved and before
// any effect runs, so cancelling costs nothing. An effect written above an asks() is a build error.
op("rename", label("Rename"), in_hand(), asks(/datum/prompt/text/rename), then(PROC_REF(apply_name)))
op("smash", hand(), confirms("Really smash it?"), then(PROC_REF(smash)))   // confirms("text") is asks(/datum/prompt/yes_no, question = "text")

/datum/prompt/text/rename
	question = "What do you want to call it?"
SCHEMA(/datum/prompt/text/rename, value, text(MAX_NAME_LEN, sanitize = SANITIZE_NAME))   // the answer is validated and sanitised before the op sees it

/obj/item/dogtag/proc/apply_name(datum/act/A)
	var/datum/prompt/text/rename/R = A.answer           // the answered request, typed
	name = R.value

// A prompt chain: each step's answer is captured under its step name, and the op resumes in its own context.
op("customise", in_hand(),
